S_{j}\neq S_{j+1}
and
i_{j}\geq i_{j+1}
for
1\leq j<k
, we have

\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}d(p_{j}^{+},p_{j+1}^{-})\geq b\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\delta_{i_{j}}\geq(b/a)\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}d(p_{j}^{-},p_{j}^{+})

and similarly
\sum_{j=k}^{n-1}d(p_{j}^{+},p_{j+1}^{-})\geq(b/a)\sum_{j=k}^{n-1}d(p_{j+1}^{-},p_{j+1}^{+})
.
Set
t:=a/(a+b)

and observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}d(p_{j}^{+},p_{j+1}^{-})

\displaystyle\geq t(b/a)\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}d(p_{j}^{-},p_{j}^{+})+(1-t)\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}d(p_{j}^{+},p_{j+1}^{-})

\displaystyle=\frac{b}{a+b}\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}d(p_{j}^{-},p_{j}^{+})+d(p_{j}^{+},p_{j+1}^{-})\geq(1+a/b)^{-1}d(p_{1}^{-},p_{k}^{-})

and
\sum_{j=k}^{n-1}d(p_{j}^{+},p_{j+1}^{-})\geq(1+a/b)^{-1}d(p_{k}^{+},p_{n}^{+})
.
Inequality (
3.9
) and the above give

\displaystyle d_{\eta}(x,y)+\epsilon

\displaystyle\geq d(x,p_{1}^{-})+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\left(\rho_{\eta}(p_{j}^{-},p_{j}^{+})+d(p_{j}^{+},p_{j+1}^{-})\right)+\rho_{\eta}(p_{n}^{-},p_{n}^{+})+d(p_{n}^{+},y)

\displaystyle\geq d(x,p_{1}^{-})+(1+a/b)^{-1}\left(d(p_{1}^{-},p_{k}^{-})+d(p_{k}^{+},p_{n}^{+})\right)+\rho_{\eta}(p_{k}^{-},p_{k}^{+})+d(p_{n}^{+},y)

\displaystyle\geq(1+a/b)^{-1}\left(d(x,p_{k}^{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{k}^{-},p_{k}^{+})+d(p_{k}^{+},y)\right),

as claimed.

Suppose now there are
x\neq y\in X
with
d_{\eta}(x,y)=0

and let
\epsilon_{j}\downarrow 0
.
By the first part of the proof,
we find
i_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
and
p_{j}^{-}\neq p_{j}^{+}\in S_{j}\in\mathcal{J}_{i_{j}}

such that
d(x,p_{j}^{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{j}^{-},p_{j}^{+})+d(p_{j}^{+},y)\leq\epsilon_{j}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\epsilon_{j}\rightarrow 0
,

(i_{j})
is unbounded, and so it has a (not relabelled) divergent subsequence.
But then

d(x,y)\leq d(x,p_{j}^{-})+d(p_{j}^{-},p_{j}^{+})+d(p_{j}^{+},y)\leq\epsilon_{j}+a\delta_{i_{j}}\rightarrow 0,

a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

For
r>0
there is
\rho(r)>0
such that
B_{\eta}(x,\rho(r))\subseteq U_{X}(x,r)
for
x\in X
.
Hence,
d
and
d_{\eta}
induce the same topology.

Proof.

Set
C:=1+2a/b
.
Since
\delta_{i}\rightarrow 0
and
\eta_{i}>0
for all
i
, for
r>0
there is
\rho=\rho(r)\in(0,r/2C)

such that
\rho_{\eta}(x,y)\leq C\rho
implies
d(x,y)\leq r/2
, for all
x,y\in X
.
Let
x\in X
,
y\in B_{\eta}(x,\rho)
, and
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
such that
d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},y)\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y)
.
Since
\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})\leq C\rho
, it holds
d(p_{-},p_{+})\leq r/2
and so

d(x,y)\leq C\rho+r/2<r
.
∎

Recall that a metric space is
proper
if closed and bounded subsets are compact.

Lemma 3.14
.

The following hold:

•

X
is complete if and only if
(X,d_{\eta})
is complete;

•

X
is proper if and only if
(X,d_{\eta})
is proper.

Proof.

From
Lemma

3.13
,
X
and
(X,d_{\eta})
have the same Cauchy sequences and topology;
this proves the first point.
For the second point, it is enough to show that sets which are bounded w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
are also bounded w.r.t.
d
.
This follows easily from
3.11
and
\sup_{i}\delta_{i}<\infty
.
∎

Definition 3.15
.

For metric space
Z,Y
, we say that a map
f\colon Z\to Y
is
David-Semmes regular
(or DS-regular)
if
there is
C\geq 1
such that
\operatorname{LIP}(f)\leq C
and for every ball
B_{Y}(y,r)\subseteq Y

there are
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in Z
,
n\leq C
, satisfying
f^{-1}(B_{Y}(y,r))\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}B_{Z}(z_{i},Cr)
.

Let
f\colon Z\to Y
be DS-regular. It follows from the definition that

(3.16)

f_{\#}\mathcal{H}_{Z}^{s}(E)\underset{C,s}{\sim}\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{s}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}f(Z)(E)

for
E\subseteq Y
and
s\in[0,\infty)
.
Note also that if
A\subseteq Z
is non-empty, then
f|_{A}
is also DS-regular, with comparable constant.
Moreover, if
\mu
is an
s
-ADR measure on
Z
and in addition
f
is surjective, then

f_{\#}\mu
is
s
-ADR on
Y
.

Definition 3.17
.

A metric space
Z
is
(metric) doubling
if there is
N\in\mathbb{N}

such that every ball can be covered by at most
N
balls of half the radius.
This is equivalent to the following: For every
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is
N(\epsilon)\in\mathbb{N}

such that if
W\subseteq B_{Z}(z,r)
is
\epsilon r
-separated, then
\#W\leq N(\epsilon)
.

For instance, a metric space
Z
supporting a doubling measure is metric doubling and, if
Z
is complete,
the converse is also true
[
LS98
]
.

One may verify that a DS-regular image of a doubling metric space is also metric doubling.

Lemma 3.18
.

Suppose
X
is metric doubling. Then the identity map
(X,d)\to(X,d_{\eta})
is David-Semmes regular.

Proof.

Set
C:=1+2a/b
.
Since
d_{\eta}\leq d
, the identity map
(X,d)\to(X,d_{\eta})
is
1
-Lipschitz.
Let
x\in X
and
r>0
.
Set
J:=\{S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}\colon a\delta_{i}\geq r,S\cap B(x,Cr)\neq\varnothing\}
.
Then

B_{\eta}(x,r)\subseteq B(x,(C+1)r)\cup\bigcup_{z\in S\in J}B(z,Cr).

Indeed, let
y\in B_{\eta}(x,r)
. If
d_{\eta}(x,y)=d(x,y)
, then
y\in B(x,(C+1)r)
.
Otherwise, let
p_{-},p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}
be such that
d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},y)\leq Cr
.
Observe that
p_{-}\in B(x,Cr)
, so
S\cap B(x,Cr)\neq\varnothing
.
If
S\in J
, then
y\in B(p_{+},Cr)
.
Otherwise,
d(x,y)\leq Cr+r
and
y\in B(x,(C+1)r)
, as required.

Since
\#S\leq M
for each
S\in\mathcal{J}
(see
3.1
),
to conclude the proof of David-Semmes regularity it only remains to show that the cardinality of
J
can be bounded independently of
x
and
r
.
For each
S\in J
, pick
z_{S}\in B(x,Cr)\cap S
and set
Z:=\{z_{S}\colon S\in J\}
. Observe that
\#Z=\#J
.
From the separation condition in
3.1
and the definition of
J
, we see that
d(z,z^{\prime})\geq(b/a)r
for
z,z^{\prime}\in Z
,
z\neq z^{\prime}
.
That is,
Z
is a
(b/a)r
-separated set contained in
B(x,Cr)
. Since
X
is metric doubling, there is a constant
N>0
,
depending only on the doubling constant of
X
and
Ca/b
, such that
\#J\leq N
.
∎

The following variant of
Lemma

3.18
will be used in
Section

5
.

Lemma 3.19
.

Suppose there is a measure
\mu
on
X
such that
(X,d,\mu)
is a metric measure space
and
\mu
vanishes on sets porous w.r.t.
d
.

Then there are countably many
\mu
-measurable sets
(E_{i})_{i}
with
\mu(X\setminus\bigcup_{i}E_{i})=0
such that
the identity map
(E_{i},d)\to(E_{i},d_{\eta})
is David-Semmes regular for each
i
(and
\eta
).

Proof.

Set
C:=1+2a/b
and
\epsilon:=b/4Ca
.
Since
\mu
vanishes on porous sets, by
[
MMPZ03
, Theorem 3.6(ii),(iv)]
,

\mu
is asymptotically doubling.
Then, by
[
Bat15
, Lemma 8.3]
, we find countably many metric doubling
\mu
-measurable sets
E_{j}

with
\mu(X\setminus\bigcup_{j}E_{j})=0
.
For
R>0
and
j
, define
g_{j,R}\colon E_{j}\to[0,1]
as

g_{j,R}(x):=\sup_{y\in U(x,R)\setminus\{x\}}\frac{d(E_{j},y)}{d(x,y)},\qquad x\in E_{j},

and observe that it is lower semi-continuous on
E_{j}
.
Also, by
Lemma

2.9
, we see that
g_{j,R}(x)\rightarrow 0
as
R\rightarrow 0
for every
j
and
\mu
-a.e.
x\in E_{j}
.
Hence,
E_{j,k}:=\{x\in E_{j}\colon g_{j,1/k}(x)\leq\epsilon/2\}
defines a countable collection of
\mu
-measurable sets which cover
\mu
-almost all of
X
.
Since
E_{j,k}
is doubling in
X
, it is in particular separable, and
we may find countably many
\mu
-measurable sets
(E_{j,k,l})_{l}
such that
E_{j,k}=\bigcup_{l}E_{j,k,l}

and
\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}E_{j,k,l}\leq\operatorname{diam}_{X}E_{j,k,l}\leq 1/Ck
for all
l
.

We claim that the identity
(E_{j,k,l},d)\to(E_{j,k,l},d_{\eta})
is DS-regular.
We argue as in
Lemma

3.18
, with minor adaptation.
Let
x\in E_{j,k,l}
and
r>0
.
If
r\geq 1/Ck
, then
B_{\eta}(x,r)\cap E_{j,k,l}=E_{j,k,l}=B_{X}(x,r)\cap E_{j,k,l}
and there is nothing to prove.
Suppose
0<r<1/Ck
, define
J\subseteq\mathcal{J}
as in the proof of
Lemma

3.18
,
and recall that

B_{\eta}(x,r)\subseteq B_{X}(x,(C+1)r)\cup\bigcup_{z\in S\in J}B_{X}(z,Cr).

Observe that to conclude the proof of the claim (and the lemma), we need only to show that
the cardinality of
J
is bounded by a constant independent of
x
and
r
.

Let
Z
as in the proof of
Lemma

3.18
, recall
that
\#Z=\#J
,

Z\subseteq B_{X}(x,Cr)
, and that
Z
is
(b/a)r
-separated in
X
.
Recall also that
Cr<1/k
and
g_{j,1/k}(x)\leq\epsilon/2
.
Since
Z\subseteq B_{X}(x,Cr)\subseteq U_{X}(x,1/k)
, for
z\in Z
there is
w_{z}\in E_{j}
such that

d(w_{z},z)\leq\epsilon d(x,z)\leq(b/4a)r
.
Since
Z
is
(b/a)r
-separated in
X
, we deduce
that
W:=\{w_{z}\colon z\in Z\}

is
(b/2a)r
-separated set in
X
and contained in

B_{X}(x,C(1+\epsilon)r)\cap E_{j}
.
Then the cardinality of
W
is bounded by a constant depending only on the doubling constant of
E_{j}

and
2aC(1+\epsilon)/b
.
Finally,
the separation condition of
Z
implies also

\#W=\#Z=\#J
, concluding the proof of the claim and hence the lemma.
∎

It turns out that, regardless of the contraction
\eta
, the separation condition in
3.1
is preserved, albeit with worse constants.

Lemma 3.20
.

There is a constant
c\in(0,1)
such that,
for
i\leq j
,
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
and
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{j}
distinct,
it holds

(3.21)

d_{\eta}(S,S^{\prime})\geq c\delta_{j}.

We may take
c=b/(1+a/b)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in S
and
y\in S^{\prime}
be such that
d_{\eta}(x,y)=d_{\eta}(S,S^{\prime})
.
If
d_{\eta}(x,y)=d(x,y)
, then
Eq.

3.21
follows from
3.1
.
Suppose
d_{\eta}(x,y)<d(x,y)
, fix
C>1+a/b
, and let
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{k}
be such that

(3.22)

d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},y)\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y).

Suppose first
1\leq k\leq j
.
If
S^{\prime}\neq S^{\prime\prime}
, then
d(p_{+},y)\geq d(S^{\prime\prime},S^{\prime})\geq b\delta_{j}
. Otherwise,
d(x,p_{-})\geq d(S,S^{\prime})\geq b\delta_{j}
.
In either case, from
Eq.

3.22
we deduce
d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq C^{-1}b\delta_{j}
.
Suppose now
k>j
. Then

d(x,y)\leq d(x,p_{-})+d(p_{+},y)
and
d(x,y)\geq b\delta_{j}
, from which
d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq C^{-1}b\delta_{j}
follows.
∎

Given
x,y\in S\in\mathcal{J}
, it is immediate that
d_{\eta}(x,y)\leq\rho_{\eta}(x,y)
, but it is not apriori clear how much smaller
d_{\eta}(x,y)
can be.
We show that it cannot be too much smaller.

Lemma 3.23
.

There is a constant
c>0

such that,
for
x,y\in S\in\mathcal{J}
,
it holds
d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq c\rho_{\eta}(x,y)
.
We may take
c=\min(2b,a_{0})/a(1+a/b)
.

Proof.

We can assume
d_{\eta}(x,y)<d(x,y)
.
Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
.
Fix
C>1+a/b
and let
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{j}
be such that

d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},y)\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y)
.
If
1\leq j\leq i
and
S\neq S^{\prime}
, then

d(S,S^{\prime})\geq b\delta_{i}

and so
Cd_{\eta}(x,y)\geq 2b\delta_{i}\geq 2(b/a)\rho_{\eta}(x,y)
.
If
S=S^{\prime}
, then
\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})\geq(a_{0}/a)\rho_{\eta}(x,y)
.
Finally, if
j>i
, then
d(x,p_{-})+d(p_{+},y)\geq d(x,y)\geq\rho_{\eta}(x,y)
.
∎

Corollary 3.24
.

Let
(X,d,\mu)
be an
s
-ADR metric measure space with shortcuts
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i}
.
Let
(\alpha_{i})
be a non-negative sequence and set

E:=\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}B_{X}(\cup\mathcal{J}_{i},\alpha_{i}\delta_{i}),\qquad E_{\eta}:=\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}B_{\eta}(\cup\mathcal{J}_{i},\alpha_{i}\delta_{i}).

Then
E
and
E_{\eta}
have full
\mu
-measure if
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{s}=\infty
, while are
\mu
-null if
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}^{s}<\infty
.

Proof.

We need only to consider
E_{\eta}
, since
E=E_{\eta}
for
\eta=(1,1,\dots)
.
Recall that, by
Lemma

3.18
,
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is
s
-ADR.
Let
c>0
be the constant of
Lemma

3.20
,
set
C:=\max(M,2/c)
, and define
\mathcal{N}_{i}:=\cup\mathcal{J}_{i}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
Lemma

3.20
and the last two conditions of
3.1

imply that
(\mathcal{N}_{i})_{i}
satisfies the assumptions of
Lemmas

2.43
and
2.44

with our choice of
C
, concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.25
.

There are
R_{0}>0
and
C\geq 1
such that
for any
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
and
0<R<R_{0}
, it holds

B_{\eta}(S,R\delta_{i})\subseteq B_{X}(S,CR\delta_{i}).

We may take
R_{0}=b/(1+a/b)
and
C=(1+a/b)^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
z\in S
and
x\in B_{\eta}(z,R\delta_{i})
.
Set
C:=1+a/b
, let
\epsilon>0
be such that
R(C+\epsilon)<b
, and pick
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{j}

such that
d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},z)\leq(C+\epsilon)d_{\eta}(x,z)
.
If
1\leq j\leq i
and
S\neq S^{\prime}
, then
b\delta_{i}\leq d(p_{+},z)\leq(C+\epsilon)R\delta_{i}
, a contradiction.
If
S=S^{\prime}
, then
p_{-}\in S
and so
d(x,S)\leq(C+\epsilon)d_{\eta}(x,z)
.
Lastly, if
j>i
,
we have
d(p_{+},z)\geq b\delta_{j}\geq(b/a)d(p_{-},p_{+})
, which yields
d(x,z)\leq(C+\epsilon)(1+a/b)d_{\eta}(x,z)
.
Hence,
d(x,S)\leq C(C+\epsilon)R\delta_{i}
for all
\epsilon>0
.
∎

Lemma 3.26
.

There is a constant
c>0
such that
the following holds for any

i\in\mathbb{N}
,
R>0
,
and
C\geq 1

with
C(R+1)\eta_{i}<c
.
Let
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
and
x\in B_{X}(p_{-},R\eta_{i}\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
,
y\in B_{X}(p_{+},R\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
.
Then
d_{\eta}(x,y)<d(x,y)
and for any
q_{-},q_{+}\in S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}
satisfying

(3.27)

d(x,q_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(q_{-},q_{+})+d(q_{+},y)\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y),

we have
q_{\pm}=p_{\pm}
.
We may take
c=\min(a_{0},b)/\max(4,a)(1+a/2b)
.

Remark 3.28
.

We stress that
Lemma

3.26
does not
guarantee the validity of
Eq.

3.27
with
q_{\pm}=p_{\pm}
,
but only that if
Eq.

3.27
holds for some
q_{\pm}
, then it must be
q_{\pm}=p_{\pm}
.
However, combined with
3.11
, it does imply
Eq.

3.27
with
q_{\pm}=p_{\pm}
and
C:=1+a/b
.
Indeed, if
C(R+1)\eta_{i}<c
, then
(C+\epsilon)(R+1)\eta_{i}<c
for all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
,
so from
3.11
and
Lemma

3.26
we deduce

d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},y)\leq(C+\epsilon)d_{\eta}(x,y),

for all
x\in B_{X}(p_{-},R\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
,
y\in B_{X}(p_{+},R\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
, and
\epsilon>0
small.

Proof.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
R>0
,
C\geq 1
,
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
, and
x\in B_{X}(p_{-},R\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
,
y\in B_{X}(p_{+},R\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
.
We show that there is
c=c(a,a_{0},b)>0
such that, if the conclusion fails, then
C(R+1)\eta_{i}\geq c
.

Since
d_{\eta}\leq d
and
\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})\leq a\delta_{i}\eta_{i}
, we have

(3.29)

d_{\eta}(x,y)\leq(2R+a)\delta_{i}\eta_{i},

and so,
if
d_{\eta}(x,y)=d(x,y)
, then

(2R+a)\delta_{i}\eta_{i}\geq d(x,y)\geq d(p_{-},p_{+})-d(x,p_{-})-d(p_{+},y)\geq a_{0}\delta_{i}-2R\delta_{i}\eta_{i},

which implies
(4R+a)\eta_{i}\geq a_{0}
.

Assume
d_{\eta}(x,y)<d(x,y)

and that
q_{-},q_{+}\in S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{j}
satisfy

Eq.

3.27
.
Suppose
1\leq j\leq i
and either
q_{-}\neq p_{-}

or
q_{+}\neq p_{+}
;
w.l.o.g.
q_{-}\neq p_{-}
.
Then

d(x,q_{-})\geq d(q_{-},p_{-})-d(x,p_{-})\geq\min(a_{0},b)\delta_{i}-R\delta_{i}\eta_{i},

Eqs.

3.27
and
3.29
imply

((2C+1)R+Ca)\eta_{i}\geq\min(a_{0},b)
.

It remains to consider the case
j>i
.
We note that

\displaystyle d(q_{-},q_{+})

\displaystyle\leq a\delta_{j}\leq\frac{a}{2b}\big(d(q_{-},p_{-})+d(q_{+},p_{+})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{a}{2b}\big(d(q_{-},x)+d(x,p_{-})+d(q_{+},y)+d(y,p_{+})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{a}{2b}\big(d(x,q_{-})+d(q_{+},y)+2R\delta_{i}\eta_{i}\big),

and by triangle inequality

(3.30)

d(x,y)\leq(1+a/2b)\big(d(x,q_{-})+d(q_{+},y)\big)+\frac{a}{2b}2R\delta_{i}\eta_{i}.

Since
p_{-}\neq p_{+}
, it holds
d(x,y)\geq a_{0}\delta_{i}-2R\delta_{i}\eta_{i}
,
and so
Eqs.

3.30
,
3.27
and
3.29
imply

(1+a/2b)(2(C+1)R+Ca)\eta_{i}\geq a_{0}
.

If
c>0
is defined as in the statement, then
C(R+1)\eta_{i}\geq c

in any of the above cases.

∎

Lemma 3.31
.

There is a constant
C\geq 1

such that the following holds for
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
R>0
with
(R+1)\eta_{i}\leq C^{-1}
.
For
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
and
0<r\leq R
, there are closed sets
\{B_{z}(r)\colon z\in S\}

satisfying:

•

B_{\eta}(S,r\delta_{i}\eta_{i})=\bigcup_{z\in S}B_{z}(r)
;

•

B_{X}(z,r\delta_{i}\eta_{i})\subseteq B_{z}(r)\subseteq B_{X}(z,Cr\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
;

•

d_{\eta}(B_{z}(r),B_{z^{\prime}}(r))\geq C^{-1}\delta_{i}\eta_{i}
and
d_{X}(B_{z}(r),B_{z^{\prime}}(r))\geq C^{-1}\delta_{i}
for
z\neq z^{\prime}\in S
.

Moreover, for
0<r<r^{\prime}\leq R
and
z\in S
, it holds
B_{z}(r)\subseteq B_{z}(r^{\prime})
.
We may take

C=\max(4,a)(1+a/b)^{4}/\min(a_{0},b).

Proof.

Set
C:=\max(4,a)(1+a/b)^{4}/\min(a_{0},b)\geq 1
and let
R>0
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
be as in the statement.
Define
C_{0}:=(1+a/b)^{2}
,

B_{z}(r):=B_{\eta}(S,r\delta_{i}\eta_{i})\cap B_{X}(z,C_{0}r\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
,
z\in S
,
and
observe that
\{B_{z}(r)\colon z\in S\}
is a collection of closed sets (
Lemma

3.13
)
satisfying the second point of the statement, because
C\geq C_{0}
.
Since
r\eta_{i}<C^{-1}<b/(1+a/b)
,
Lemma

3.25

shows that
\{B_{z}(r)\colon z\in S\}
covers
B_{\eta}(S,r\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
.
Let
z\neq z^{\prime}\in S
.
Let
c_{0}\in(0,1)
be defined as in
Lemma

3.26
and note
that
(C_{0}r+1)\eta_{i}<C_{0}C^{-1}<c_{0}/(1+a/b)
.
Then, by
3.28
, for
x\in B_{z}(r)
and
y\in B_{z^{\prime}}(r)
we have

(1+a/b)d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq d(x,z)+\rho_{\eta}(z,z^{\prime})+d(z^{\prime},y)\geq a_{0}\delta_{i}\eta_{i},

which implies
d_{\eta}(B_{z}(r),B_{z^{\prime}}(r))\geq C^{-1}\delta_{i}\eta_{i}
because
C^{-1}\leq a_{0}/(1+a/b)
.
If
x,y
are as above, then

d(x,y)\geq d(z,z^{\prime})-d(x,z)-d(z^{\prime},y)\geq a_{0}\delta_{i}-2C_{0}R\delta_{i}\eta_{i}\geq(a_{0}-2C_{0}C^{-1})\delta_{i}\geq C^{-1}\delta_{i}

concludes the proof.
∎

The following was already proven in
[
LDLR17
, Theorem 1.3]

under slightly more restrictive assumption (see
Section

A.1
).
We include it here for comparison with, and as warm-up to,
4.3
and
4.1
.
Our proof relies on the Borel-Cantelli-type lemmas of
Section

2.6
and
the properties of
d_{\eta}
developed so far.

Proposition 3.32
.

Let
(X,d,\mu)
be an
s
-ADR metric measure space with shortcuts
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i}

and
\eta=(\eta_{i})\subseteq(0,1]
.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

there is a positive-measure
\mu
-measurable subset of
X

on which
d
and
d_{\eta}
are biLipschitz equivalent;

•

\inf\{\eta_{i}\colon i\in\mathbb{N}\}>0
;

•

d
and
d_{\eta}
are biLipschitz equivalent on
X
.

Proof.

We need only to prove that the first point implies the second.
We prove the contrapositive.
Suppose
\inf\{\eta_{i}\colon i\in\mathbb{N}\}=0
, let
(\eta_{i_{j}})_{j}
and

(\alpha_{j})\subseteq[1,\infty)
be such that
\alpha_{j}\eta_{i_{j}}\rightarrow 0
and
\sum_{j}(\alpha_{j}\eta_{i_{j}})^{s}=\infty
.
Set
E_{0}:=\limsup_{j}B_{X}(\cup\mathcal{J}_{i_{j}},\alpha_{j}\eta_{i_{j}}\delta_{i_{j}})
and
fix a positive-measure
\mu
-measurable set
E\subseteq X
.
By
Lemma

3.18
, the measure
\mu
is
s
-ADR (in particular doubling)
also on
(X,d_{\eta})
. Since,
by
3.24
,
E_{0}

has full measure,
we may then find a Lebesgue density point
x
of
E
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
with
x\in E\cap E_{0}
.

Since
x\in E_{0}
, there are a subsequence
(i_{j_{k}})_{k}
and
p_{k}^{-}\in S_{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{i_{j_{k}}}

such that
d(x,p_{k}^{-})\leq 2\alpha_{j_{k}}\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}\delta_{i_{j_{k}}}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
C_{0}\geq 1
denote the constant of
Lemma

3.31

and observe that, since
(\alpha_{j_{k}}+1)\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}\rightarrow 0
,
we may assume w.l.o.g.
(2\alpha_{j_{k}}+1)\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}\leq C_{0}^{-1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\{B_{z,k}\colon z\in S_{k}\}

be as in
Lemma

3.31

with
r=R=2\alpha_{j_{k}}

(and
i=i_{j_{k}}
,
S=S_{k}
),
let
p_{k}^{+}\in S_{k}\setminus\{p_{k}^{-}\}
, set
B_{k}^{-}:=B_{p_{k}^{-},k}
and
B_{k}^{+}:=B_{p_{k}^{+},k}
,
and observe that
x\in B_{k}^{-}
.
From

d_{\eta}(p_{k}^{+},x)\leq(2\alpha_{j_{k}}+a)\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}\delta_{i_{j_{k}}}\leq(2+a)\alpha_{j_{k}}\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}\delta_{i_{j_{k}}}
,
we have

(3.33)

B_{k}^{+}\subseteq B_{\eta}(S_{k},2\alpha_{j_{k}}\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}\delta_{i_{j_{k}}})\subseteq B_{\eta}(x,(4+a)\alpha_{j_{k}}\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}\delta_{i_{j_{k}}}).

By
Lemma

3.31
, we have

\mu(B_{k}^{+})\sim(\alpha_{j_{k}}\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}\delta_{i_{j_{k}}})^{s}
.
Then, since
x
is a Lebesgue density point of
E
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
,
from the above and
Eq.

3.33

we see that
E\cap B_{k}^{+}\neq\varnothing
for all sufficiently large
k
.
Assume w.l.o.g.
E\cap B_{k}^{+}\neq\varnothing
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}

and
let
y_{k}\in E\cap B_{k}^{+}
.
Then
Eq.

3.33
implies

d_{\eta}(x,y_{k})\leq(4+a)\alpha_{j_{k}}\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}\delta_{i_{j_{k}}}
,
while

Lemma

3.31
and
x\in B_{k}^{-}

give

d(x,y_{k})\geq C_{0}^{-1}\delta_{i_{j_{k}}}
.
We finally have

\limsup_{E\ni y\rightarrow x}\frac{d(x,y)}{d_{\eta}(x,y)}\geq\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{d(x,y_{k})}{d_{\eta}(x,y_{k})}\geq\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{C_{0}^{-1}}{(4+a)\alpha_{j_{k}}\eta_{i_{j_{k}}}}=\infty.

∎

4.
Shortcut metric spaces and PI (un)rectifiability

In this section we determine the sequences
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
for which a shortcut space
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is PI rectifiable or purely PI unrectifiable, and show that there is no other possibility.

4.1.
Pure PI unrectifiability

The main theorem regarding pure PI unrectifiability of shortcut spaces is the following.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(X,d,\mu)
be an
s
-ADR metric measure space with shortcuts and
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
.
Suppose
\inf\{\eta_{i}\colon i\notin I\}=0
whenever
\sum_{i\in I}\eta_{i}^{s}<\infty
,
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.
Then
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is purely PI unrectifiable.

In particular, if
\eta_{i}\rightarrow 0
, then
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is purely PI unrectifiable whenever
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\eta_{i}^{s}=\infty
.

For the proof of
4.1
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
X
be a metric space,
E,F\subseteq X
sets, and let
\gamma\colon C\to E\cup F

be a continuous function from a non-empty closed set
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.
Suppose
\gamma(C)\cap E\neq\varnothing
and
\gamma(C)\cap F\neq\varnothing
.
Then
\operatorname{gap}\gamma\geq d(E,F)
.

Proof.

We may assume w.l.o.g.
d(E,F)>0
,
0\in C\subseteq[0,\infty)
, and
\gamma(0)\in E
.
Set

\alpha:=\max\{t\in C\colon\gamma([0,t]\cap C)\subseteq E\}.

It is not difficult to see that
C\cap(\alpha,\alpha+\epsilon)=\varnothing
for some
\epsilon>0
.
Also, by assumption
\gamma^{-1}(F)\cap C\neq\varnothing
, and therefore
C\setminus[0,\alpha]
is a non-empty closed set which is bounded from below.
We can therefore define
\beta:=\min(C\setminus[0,\alpha])
.
By definition of
\alpha
, it must be
\gamma(\beta)\in F
, and thus

\operatorname{gap}\gamma\geq d(\gamma(\alpha),\gamma(\beta))\geq d(E,F)
.
∎

Proof of
4.1
.

By
Lemma

2.22
, there is an subsequence
(\eta_{i_{j}})_{j}
with
\eta_{i_{j}}\rightarrow 0
and
\sum_{j}\eta_{i_{j}}^{s}=\infty
.
Define

E_{0}:=\limsup_{j\rightarrow\infty}B_{\eta}(\cup\mathcal{J}_{i_{j}},\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}}),

and note that by
3.24
it has full measure.
Let
E\subseteq X
be a positive-measure
\mu
-measurable set,
x\in E_{0}\cap E
a Lebesgue density point of
E
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
,

C_{0}\geq 1
the constant in
Lemma

3.31
,
and
0<\delta\leq 1/C_{0}(4+a)
.
Let
C\geq 1
and
\epsilon,r>0
.
We claim that there is
y\in B_{\eta}(x,r)\cap E
,
y\neq x
, such that

(x,y)
is not
(C,\delta,\epsilon)
-connected in
(E,d_{\eta},\mu)
; see
2.34
.
By
2.37
, this will conclude the proof.

Let
R\geq C(4+a)
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
d_{\eta}(x,\cup\mathcal{J}_{i_{j}})\leq\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}}
,
(R+1)\eta_{i_{j}}\leq C_{0}^{-1}
,
and
(4+a)\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}}\leq r
.
Let
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i_{j}}
be such that
d_{\eta}(x,S)\leq 2\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}}
and let
\{B_{z}(t)\colon z\in S\}

be as in
Lemma

3.31
for
0<t\leq R
.
Since
x
is a Lebesgue density point of
E
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
,
we may assume that
j\in\mathbb{N}
was taken so large that
B_{z}(2)\cap E\neq\varnothing
for all
z\in S
.
Indeed, if this failed for infinitely many
j
,

B_{X}(z,2\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}})\subseteq B_{z}(2)\subseteq B_{\eta}(x,4\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}})

and
s
-AD regularity of
\mu
on
(X,d)
and
(X,d_{\eta})
would contradict the fact that
x

is a Lebesgue density point of
E
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
.

Let
z_{0}\in S
be such that
x\in B_{z_{0}}(2)
, pick
z\in S\setminus\{z_{0}\}
,
y\in B_{z}(2)\cap E
, and
observe that
d_{\eta}(x,y)\leq(4+a)\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}}\leq r
, i.e.
y\in B_{\eta}(x,r)\cap E
.

Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be a non-empty compact set and
\gamma\colon K\to X
a continuous
map with
\gamma(\min K)=x
and
\gamma(\max K)=y
.
If
\operatorname{var}_{\eta}\gamma>R\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}}
, the choices of
R
and
y
imply

\operatorname{var}_{\eta}\gamma>C(4+a)\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}}\geq Cd_{\eta}(x,y).

Suppose
\operatorname{var}_{\eta}\gamma\leq R\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}}
.
Then
\gamma(K)\subseteq B_{\eta}(S,R\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}})
,
\gamma(K)\cap B_{z_{0}}(R)\neq\varnothing
,
and

\gamma(K)\cap\bigcup\{B_{z^{\prime}}(R)\colon z^{\prime}\in S\setminus\{z_{0}\}\}\neq\varnothing
.
From
Lemmas

4.2
and
3.31
, we deduce

\operatorname{gap}_{\eta}\gamma\geq d_{\eta}\left(B_{z_{0}}(R),\bigcup\{B_{z^{\prime}}(R)\colon z^{\prime}\in S\setminus\{z_{0}\}\}\right)\geq C_{0}^{-1}\delta_{i_{j}}\eta_{i_{j}}\geq\delta d_{\eta}(x,y).

This proves the claim and hence the thesis.
∎

4.2.
PI rectifiability

In this subsection we prove the following.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
(X,d,\mu)
be a PI rectifiable
s
-ADR metric measure space with shortcuts and
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
.
Suppose there is
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
\sum_{i\in I}\eta_{i}^{s}<\infty
and
\inf\{\eta_{i}\colon i\notin I\}>0
.
Then
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is PI rectifiable.

In particular, if
\eta_{i}\rightarrow 0
, then
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is PI rectifiable whenever
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\eta_{i}^{s}<\infty
.

Remark 4.4
.

The hypotheses on the sequence
\eta
in
4.1
and
4.3
exactly complement each other.
Therefore, for a given PI rectifiable
s
-ADR metric measure space
(X,d,\mu)
with shortcuts, these results characterise when the shortcut metric space is PI rectifiable or purely PI unrectifiable.

Definition 4.5
.

Given a metric space
X
with shortcuts
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i}

and
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
, we set

(4.6)

\mathrm{Bad}:=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}B_{\eta}(\cup\mathcal{J}_{i},\alpha\delta_{i}\eta_{i}).

One may verify that

\mathrm{Bad}
may be equivalently defined replacing the set
B_{\eta}(\cup\mathcal{J}_{i},\alpha\delta_{i}\eta_{i})

with
B_{X}(\cup\mathcal{J}_{i},\alpha\delta_{i}\eta_{i})
.

For
4.3
, we need only to focus
on points
not
in
\mathrm{Bad}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
X
be a metric space with shortcuts
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i}
, let
a,b
be as in
3.1
,
and
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
.
For any constant
C>1+a/b
,

x\notin\mathrm{Bad}
, and
\epsilon>0
,
there is
R>0
with the following property.
If
y\in B_{\eta}(x,R)
and
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}
satisfy

d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},y)\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y),

then

\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})\leq\epsilon d_{\eta}(x,y).

Proof.

Define
\alpha:=aC/\epsilon
.
Since
x\in X\setminus\mathrm{Bad}
, there is
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

d_{\eta}(x,\cup\mathcal{J}_{i})\geq\alpha\delta_{i}\eta_{i},\qquad i\geq i_{0}.

Let
R>0
be such that
CR<a_{0}\delta_{i}\eta_{i}
for
1\leq i<i_{0}
,
where
a_{0}
is as in
3.1
.
Let
y
and
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
be as in the statement.
Then
a_{0}\delta_{i}\eta_{i}\leq CR
and,
from the choice of
R
, it follows that
i\geq i_{0}
and thus

\alpha\delta_{i}\eta_{i}\leq d_{\eta}(x,p_{-})\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y)
.
Then

\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})\leq a\delta_{i}\eta_{i}\leq(a/\alpha)Cd_{\eta}(x,y)=\epsilon d_{\eta}(x,y).

∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(X,d,\mu)
be an
s
-ADR metric measure space with shortcuts.
There are constants
C_{0}\geq 1
and
c_{0}\in(0,1)
such that the following holds.
Let
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
be a sequence,

E\subseteq X\setminus\mathrm{Bad}
a
\mu
-measurable set,
and suppose
(X,d,\mu)
is
(C,\delta,\epsilon,r)
-connected along
E

for some
C\geq 1
,
\delta,\epsilon\in(0,1)
, and
r>0
.
Then, for every Lebesgue density point
x
of
E
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
,
there is
r_{x}>0

such that
(x,y)
is
(\overline{C},\overline{\delta},\overline{\epsilon})
-connected
in
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
for all
y\in B_{\eta}(x,r_{x})\setminus\{x\}
,
where

\overline{C}=C_{0}C
,
\overline{\delta}=C_{0}\delta
,
and

\overline{\epsilon}=c_{0}\delta^{s}\epsilon
.

Proof.

Let
a,b
be as in
3.1
,
fix
\tilde{C}_{0}>1+a/b
, set
C_{0}:=7\tilde{C}_{0}
, and let
c_{0}\in(0,1)
to be determined later.
Let
R_{x}>0
be the radius given by
Lemma

4.7
applied to
x
with

\epsilon\equiv\delta
and
C\equiv\tilde{C}_{0}
.
Fix
0<\alpha<\min(\delta,1/2)
.
Since
x
is a Lebesgue point of
E
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
, there is
0<r_{x}<\min(r,R_{x})/\tilde{C}_{0}(1+\alpha)
such
that for every
y\in B_{\eta}(x,r_{x})\setminus\{x\}
there is
z\in B_{\eta}(y,\alpha d_{\eta}(x,y))\cap E
.
We claim that the thesis holds with this value of
r_{x}
,
C_{0}
, and
c_{0}
sufficiently small.

Let
y
and
z
be as above, set
\rho_{y}:=d_{\eta}(x,y)
,
\rho_{z}:=d_{\eta}(x,z)
,
and observe that
(1-\alpha)\rho_{y}\leq\rho_{z}\leq(1+\alpha)\rho_{y}
.
Let
A\subseteq X
be a
\mu
-measurable set with

\mu(A\cap B_{\eta}(x,C_{0}C\rho_{y}))<c_{0}\delta^{s}\epsilon\,\mu(B_{\eta}(x,C_{0}C\rho_{y})).

Suppose first
\rho_{z}=d(x,z)
.
Then
B_{X}(x,C\rho_{z})\subseteq B_{\eta}(x,C(1+\alpha)\rho_{y})\subseteq B_{\eta}(x,C_{0}C\rho_{y})

and
\rho_{z}\geq\rho_{y}/2
imply
for
c_{0}\in(0,1)
small enough

\mu(A\cap B_{X}(x,C\rho_{z}))<c_{0}\delta^{s}\epsilon\,\mu(B_{\eta}(x,C_{0}C\rho_{y}))\lesssim(c_{0}\epsilon)(C\rho_{z})^{s}\leq\epsilon\mu(B_{X}(x,C\rho_{z})),

where we have also used the fact that
\mu
is
s
-ADR also on
(X,d_{\eta})
, see
Lemma

3.18
.
Since
x\in E
and
d(x,z)=\rho_{z}\leq(1+\alpha)\rho_{y}<r
, the connectivity assumption
on
E
yields a curve fragment
\gamma_{z}
from
x
to
z
, satisfying
\operatorname{var}_{X}\gamma_{z}\leq C\rho_{z}
,

\operatorname{gap}_{X}\gamma_{z}<\delta\rho_{z}
, and which may meet
A
only at the endpoints of its domain.
The curve fragment
\gamma
, obtained following
\gamma_{z}
and then jumping from
z
to
y
, satisfies

\displaystyle\operatorname{var}_{\eta}\gamma

\displaystyle\leq C\rho_{z}+\alpha\rho_{y}\leq(C(1+\alpha)+\alpha)\rho_{y}<C_{0}C\rho_{y},

\displaystyle\operatorname{gap}_{\eta}\gamma

\displaystyle<\delta\rho_{z}+\alpha\rho_{y}\leq(2+\alpha)\delta\rho_{y}<C_{0}\delta\rho_{y},

and may meet
A
at its endpoints or in
z
.
It is not difficult to see that restricting the domain of
\gamma
we can find a curve fragment
\tilde{\gamma}

from
x
to
y
having slightly larger gap and variation (in
d_{\eta}
) which does not intersect
z
.

Suppose now
\rho_{z}<d(x,z)
, let
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}
be such that

d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},z)\leq\tilde{C}_{0}\rho_{z},

and recall that, by the choice of
r_{x}
, we have
\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})\leq\delta\rho_{z}
.
We first construct curve fragments
\gamma_{x}
,
\gamma_{z}
from
x
to
p_{-}
and
z
to
p_{+}
, respectively,
with controlled variation and gap.
Let
(w,p)
be one of the pairs
(x,p_{-})
,
(z,p_{+})
.
Suppose
t:=d(w,p)\geq\delta\rho_{z}
.
Since
t\leq\tilde{C}_{0}\rho_{z}
and
d_{\eta}(x,w)\leq\rho_{z}
, we have

B_{X}(w,Ct)\subseteq B_{\eta}(x,(\tilde{C}_{0}C+1)\rho_{z})\subseteq B_{\eta}(x,C_{0}C\rho_{y})
and
therefore

(4.9)

\displaystyle\mu(A\cap B_{X}(w,Ct))

\displaystyle<c_{0}\delta^{s}\epsilon\,\mu(B_{\eta}(x,C_{0}C\rho_{y}))\lesssim(c_{0}\epsilon)(C\delta\rho_{z})^{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim(c_{0}\epsilon)(Ct)^{s}\leq\epsilon\mu(B_{X}(w,Ct)),

provided
c_{0}\in(0,1)
is taken small enough.
Then,
t\leq\tilde{C}_{0}\rho_{z}\leq\tilde{C}_{0}(1+\alpha)\rho_{y}<r
,
w\in E
, the connectivity assumption on
E
,
and
Eq.

4.9
ensure the
existence of a curve fragment
\gamma_{w}

from
w
to
p
satisfying

(4.10)

\displaystyle\operatorname{var}_{\eta}\gamma_{w}

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{var}_{X}\gamma_{w}\leq Ct\leq\tilde{C}_{0}C\rho_{z},

\displaystyle\operatorname{gap}_{\eta}\gamma_{w}

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{gap}_{X}\gamma_{w}<\delta t\leq\tilde{C}_{0}\delta\rho_{z},

and, moreover,
\gamma_{w}
may meet
A
only at the endpoints of its domain.
If, instead,
t=d(w,p)<\delta\rho_{z}
, let
\gamma_{w}\colon\{0,1\}\to X

be given by
\gamma_{w}(0):=w
,
\gamma_{w}(1):=p
,
and observe that it
satisfies
Eq.

4.10
and avoids
A
in the same way.
Then, the curve fragment
\gamma\colon K\to X
which follows
\gamma_{x}
, then

\gamma_{z}
with reverse orientation,
and, if
z\neq y
, finally jumps from
z
to
y
,
satisfies

(4.11)

\displaystyle\operatorname{var}_{\eta}\gamma

\displaystyle\leq(2\tilde{C}_{0}C+\delta)\rho_{z}+\alpha\rho_{y}<C_{0}C\rho_{y},

\displaystyle\operatorname{gap}_{\eta}\gamma

\displaystyle\leq(2\tilde{C}_{0}+1)\delta\rho_{z}+\alpha\rho_{y}<C_{0}\delta\rho_{y},

where we have used
Eq.

4.10
,
\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})\leq\delta\rho_{z}
, and
d_{\eta}(z,y)\leq\delta\rho_{y}
.
Note that
\gamma
may meet
A
only at its endpoints or at
p_{-},p_{+}
, or
z
.
By construction, there are
t_{-}<t_{+}<t_{z}\in K
such that
\gamma^{-1}\{p_{-},p_{+},z\}=\{t_{-},t_{+},t_{z}\}
.
Hence, there is a curve fragment
\tilde{\gamma}
, obtained restricting slightly the domain of
\gamma
,
which may meet
A
only at its endpoints, and moreover still satisfies
Eq.

4.11
.
∎

Proof of
4.3
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
be such that
\sum_{i\in I}\eta_{i}^{s}<\infty
and
\inf\{\eta_{i}\colon i\notin I\}>0
.
If
I
is empty or finite, then
d
and
d_{\eta}
are biLipschitz and
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is trivially PI rectifiable.
Assume
I
is infinite and suppose we have established the thesis for
I=\mathbb{N}
.
Let
(i_{j})_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
be the strictly increasing sequence with
I=\{i_{j}\colon j\in\mathbb{N}\}

and observe that

d_{\eta}
is biLipschitz to the distance
d_{\tilde{\eta}}
obtained from the shortcuts
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i_{j}},\delta_{i_{j}})\}_{j}

and the sequence
\tilde{\eta}_{j}:=\eta_{i_{j}}
.
That is,
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is biLipschitz to the PI rectifiable space
(X,d_{\tilde{\eta}},\mu)
.
Hence, we need only to consider the case
I=\mathbb{N}
.

By
3.24
and
I=\mathbb{N}
, we have
\mu(\mathrm{Bad})=0
.
Let
C_{0},c_{0}
be as in
Lemma

4.8
and let
\delta\in(0,1)
.
Since
(X,d,\mu)
is PI rectifiable, by
2.38
and

Lemma

2.11
, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X

there are
(C_{x},\epsilon_{x},r_{x})
as in
2.35
with
\delta\equiv\delta/C_{0}
.
We may also assume
x\mapsto(C_{x},\epsilon_{x},r_{x})
to be Borel measurable; see
Lemma

2.39
.
Then,
there are countably many disjoint Borel sets
E_{n}\subseteq X
and constants
(C_{n},\epsilon_{n},r_{n})

such that
(X,d,\mu)
is
(C_{n},\epsilon_{n},\delta/C_{0},r_{n})
-connected along
E_{n}

and
\mu(X\setminus\bigcup_{n}E_{n})=0
.
Then, by
Lemma

4.8
, for every
n
and
\mu
-a.e.
x\in E_{n}
there
are constants
(\tilde{C}_{x},\tilde{\epsilon}_{x},\tilde{r}_{x})

such that
(x,y)
is
(\tilde{C}_{x},\delta,\tilde{\epsilon}_{x})
-connected in
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
for
y\in B_{\eta}(x,\tilde{r}_{x})\setminus\{x\}
.
By
2.38
we conclude that
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is PI rectifiable.
∎

5.
Shortcut metric spaces and Lipschitz differentiability

Let
Y
be a Banach space,
(X,d,\mu)
a
Y
-LDS with shortcuts, and
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
Lipschitz.
In this section we give a precise description of the set of points in
X
where the derivative of
f
on
(X,d)
defines a derivative of
f
on
(X,d_{\eta})
.
We also show that this is the only way in which a differentiable structure on
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
can arise, see
Lemma

5.3
and
5.7
.
We require some definitions in order to state the main result in
5.6
.

Definition 5.1

(Compatible differentiable structure)
.

Let
Y
be a Banach space,

(X,d,\mu)
a
Y
-LDS, and suppose
(X,d)
has shortcuts
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i}
.
We say that a Cheeger atlas

\{(U_{j},\varphi_{j}\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}})\}_{j}

of
(X,d,\mu)
is
compatible
with the shortcuts if

(5.2)

\operatorname{diam}\varphi_{j}(S)=0

for each
j
and
S\in\mathcal{J}
.
If
(X,d,\mu)
has a compatible atlas, we then say that it is a
Y
-LDS with compatible shortcuts,
or
that the shortcuts and the differentiable structure are compatible.

Under the compatibility condition of
5.1
,
the only possible differentiable structure on
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is the one of
(X,d,\mu)
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
(X,d,\mu)
be an LDS
with compatible shortcuts.
Let
Y
be a non-zero Banach space,
E\subseteq X
a positive-measure
\mu
-measurable set,
and suppose
(E,d_{\eta},\mu)
is a
Y
-LDS.
Then, the restriction to
E
of any compatible atlas of
(X,d,\mu)
is
an atlas of
(E,d_{\eta},\mu)
.

Proof.

The proof consists of standard reductions and
the combination of
Lemma

3.19
with
[
CEB23
, Theorem 3.26]
.

Since the differentiable structure of an LDS does not depend on the target (see
Lemma

2.6
),
it is enough to consider the case
Y=\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\{(U_{j},\varphi_{j}\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}})\}_{j}
be a compatible atlas,
observe that
\varphi_{j}
is Lipschitz also w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
,
and let
j
be such that
\mu(E\cap U_{j})>0
.
If
n_{j}=0
, then by
2.17
and
Lemma

3.13

\mu
-a.e.
x\in U_{j}

is isolated and therefore
(E\cap U_{j},\varphi_{j})=(E\cap U_{j},0)
is trivially a chart.
Suppose now
n_{j}\geq 1
.
By
Lemmas

2.12
and
3.19
and
inner regularity of
\mu
, there are countably many compact sets
K_{j,k}\subseteq E\cap U_{j}

with
\mu(E\cap U_{j}\setminus\bigcup_{k}K_{j,k})=0

and such that the identity map
\iota|_{K_{j,k}}\colon(K_{j,k},d)\to(K_{j,k},d_{\eta})
is DS-regular.
Then, for each
k
,
(K_{j,k},d,\mu)
and
(K_{j,k},d_{\eta},\mu)
are compact (hence proper) LDS,
\mu
is Radon,

\iota|_{K_{j,k}}\colon(K_{j,k},d)\to(K_{j,k},d_{\eta})
is a surjective DS-regular map preserving
\mu
,

\varphi_{j}|_{K_{j,k}}\colon(K_{j,k},d_{\eta})\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}
Lipschitz,
and
\varphi_{j}|_{K_{j,k}}\circ\iota|_{K_{j,k}}\colon(K_{j,k},d)\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}
is a global chart on
(K_{j,k},d,\mu)
.
Thus, by
[
CEB23
, Theorem 3.26]
,
\varphi_{j}|_{K_{j,k}}

is a chart on
(K_{j,k},d_{\eta},\mu)
.

Then, for any Lipschitz
f\colon(E,d_{\eta})\to\mathbb{R}
, any
j,k
, and
\mu
-a.e.
x\in K_{j,k}
, there is a unique
linear map
T_{x}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\operatorname{Lip}_{\eta}((f-T_{x}\circ\varphi_{j})|_{K_{j,k}};x)=0
.
Since
(E,d_{\eta},\mu)
is an LDS, by
Lemmas

2.12
and
2.9

we conclude
\operatorname{Lip}_{\eta}((f-T_{x}\circ\varphi_{j})|_{E};x)=0
at
\mu
-a.e.
x\in E\cap U_{j}
, and any
j
.

Uniqueness of the
\varphi_{j}
-differentials follows from

\operatorname{Lip}_{\eta}(\langle v,\varphi_{j}\rangle|_{E};x)\geq\operatorname{Lip}(\langle v,\varphi_{j}\rangle|_{E};x)=\operatorname{Lip}(\langle v,\varphi_{j}\rangle;x)>0

for
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}\setminus\{0\}

and
\mu
-a.e.
x\in E\cap U_{j}
;
see
Lemmas

2.5
,
2.9
and
2.12
.
The first inequality holds because
d_{\eta}\leq d
.
∎

We now introduce a definition related to the set Bad given in
4.5
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
Y
be a Banach space,

(X,d,\mu)
be a
Y
-LDS with compatible shortcuts
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i}
, and
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
a map.
For
\epsilon>0
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, gather in
\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\epsilon}(f)
the shortcuts
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
for which

\operatorname{diam}f(S)\geq\epsilon\eta_{i}\delta_{i}
.
We define

(5.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f)

\displaystyle:=\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}B_{\eta}(\cup\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\epsilon}(f),\eta_{i}\delta_{i}/\epsilon),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Bad}(f)

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{\epsilon>0}\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f).

If
0<\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
, then
\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}(f)
.
Hence,
\operatorname{Bad}(f)=\bigcup_{j}\operatorname{Bad}_{1/j}(f)

shows that
\operatorname{Bad}(f)
is Borel.
We show that, if
f
is Lipschitz on
(X,d_{\eta})
,
\operatorname{Bad}(f)
coincides with the set of non-differentiability points of
f
, up to a null set.

The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
Y
be a Banach space,

(X,d,\mu)
a
Y
-LDS with compatible shortcuts,
and suppose
\mu
vanishes on sets porous w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
.
Then, for any Lipschitz map
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
, we have

•

f
is
\mu
-a.e. differentiable on
X\setminus\operatorname{Bad}(f)
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
and any compatible atlas;

•

f
is
\mu
-a. nowhere differentiable on any subset of
\operatorname{Bad}(f)
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
and any compatible atlas.

Moreover, the Cheeger differentials of
f
w.r.t.
d
and
d_{\eta}
agree
\mu
-a.e. on
X\setminus\operatorname{Bad}(f)
.

Before proving
5.6
, let us single out two immediate applications.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
Y
be a non-zero Banach space and
(X,d,\mu)
a

Y
-LDS with compatible shortcuts.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is a
Y
-LDS;

•

\mu(\operatorname{Bad}(f))=0
for every Lipschitz map
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y

and
\mu
vanishes on sets porous w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
.

Proof.

Suppose
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is a
Y
-LDS.
Then, by
Lemma

2.12
,
sets porous w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
are
\mu
-null.
By
Lemma

5.3
, there is a compatible atlas of
(X,d,\mu)
which restricts
to an atlas of
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)

and, by
5.6
, we then have
\mu(\operatorname{Bad}(f))=0
for any Lipschitz
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
.
Conversely, if the assumptions of the second point are satisfied, then
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is a
Y
-LDS by
5.6
.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
Y
be a non-zero Banach space,
(X,d,\mu)

a
Y
-LDS with compatible shortcuts,
and suppose
\mu
vanishes on sets porous w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
.
Let
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
be a Lipschitz map
and
E\subseteq\operatorname{Bad}(f)
a positive-measure
\mu
-measurable set.
Then
(E,d_{\eta},\mu)
is not a
Y
-LDS.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that
(E,d_{\eta},\mu)
is a
Y
-LDS.
By
Lemma

5.3
, there is a compatible atlas of
(X,d,\mu)

which restricts to an atlas of
(E,d_{\eta},\mu)
.
But then, by
5.6
,
f|_{E}\colon(E,d_{\eta})\to Y
is
\mu
-almost nowhere
differentiable on
E
, from which we conclude
\mu(E)=0
.
∎

We now turn to the proof of
5.6
.
Unsurprisingly (in view of
2.38
),
the proof of
5.6
is reminiscent
of the ones of
4.3
and
4.1
.
The crucial point is that
\mathrm{Bad}(f)
may be much smaller than
\mathrm{Bad}
or,
in other words,
Lipschitz maps
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y

may be unable to fully capture the lack of ‘connectivity’ (in the sense of
2.35
)
of the underlying space.
This is the fundamental phenomenon behind
1.1
.

8.3
and
the constructions of non-differentiable maps in
Section

10

also serve to illustrative this principle.

The following lemma should be compared to
Lemma

4.7
.
It will be used for the proof of the first claim in
5.6
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space with shortcuts
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i}

and
C>1+a/b
, where
a,b
are as in
3.1
.
Then, for every

\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
, Banach space
Y
,
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
Lipschitz,

x\notin\operatorname{Bad}(f)
, and
\epsilon>0
, there is
R>0
with the following property.
If
y\in B_{\eta}(x,R)
and
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}
satisfy

d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},y)\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y),

then

(5.10)

\|f(p_{-})-f(p_{+})\|_{Y}\leq\epsilon d_{\eta}(x,y).

Proof.

Set
L:=\operatorname{LIP}_{\eta}(f)
and
\epsilon_{0}:=\epsilon\min(a_{0},1/aL)/C
, where
a_{0}
is as in
3.1
.
Since
x\notin\operatorname{Bad}(f)
, in particular
x\notin\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon_{0}}(f)
, we find
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(5.11)

d_{\eta}(x,\cup\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\epsilon_{0}}(f))\geq\eta_{i}\delta_{i}/\epsilon_{0},

for
i\geq i_{0}
.
Let
R>0
be such that
a_{0}\eta_{i}\delta_{i}\leq CR
implies
i\geq i_{0}
.
Let
y
and
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
be as in the statement
and observe that

a_{0}\eta_{i}\delta_{i}\leq\rho_{\eta}(p_{-}p_{+})\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y)\leq CR,

and so, by the choice of
R
, we have
i\geq i_{0}
and hence
Eq.

5.11
holds.
If
S\notin\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\epsilon_{0}}(f)
, then

\|f(p_{-})-f(p_{+})\|_{Y}\leq\epsilon_{0}\eta_{i}\delta_{i}\leq\epsilon_{0}(C/a_{0})d_{\eta}(x,y)\leq\epsilon d_{\eta}(x,y).

Otherwise,
Eq.

5.11
gives

\eta_{i}\delta_{i}\leq\epsilon_{0}d_{\eta}(x,\cup\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\epsilon_{0}}(f))\leq\epsilon_{0}d(x,S)\leq\epsilon_{0}Cd_{\eta}(x,y)

and, since
f
is
L
-Lipschitz on
(X,d_{\eta})
, we finally have

\|f(p_{-})-f(p_{+})\|_{Y}\leq La\eta_{i}\delta_{i}\leq\epsilon_{0}aLCd_{\eta}(x,y)\leq\epsilon d_{\eta}(x,y).

∎

We require the following lemma for the proof of the non-differentiability claim in
5.6
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
(X,d)
be a metric space with shortcuts
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i}
and let
\eta=(\eta_{i})\subseteq(0,1]
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose
\varphi\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a function satisfying
\operatorname{diam}\varphi(S)=0
for
S\in\mathcal{J}
.
Then, for any Banach space
Y
,
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
, and
x\in\operatorname{Bad}(f)
, there are a constant
c>0

and a sequence
(x_{j})\subseteq X\setminus\{x\}
,
x_{j}\rightarrow x
, such that

\limsup_{j\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|f(x_{j})-f(x)-T(\varphi(x_{j})-\varphi(x))\|_{Y}}{d_{\eta}(x,x_{j})}\geq c,

for every linear
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to Y
.

Proof.

Since
x\in\operatorname{Bad}(f)
there are
\epsilon>0
,
a strictly increasing sequence
(i_{k})\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, and
S_{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{i_{k}}^{\epsilon}(f)
such that
d_{\eta}(x,S_{k})\leq 2\eta_{i_{k}}\delta_{i_{k}}/\epsilon
.
Since
d_{\eta}(z,w)\leq a\eta_{i_{k}}\delta_{i_{k}}
for
z,w\in S_{k}
, possibly shrinking
\epsilon>0
, we may assume

(5.13)

d_{\eta}(x,p)\leq\eta_{i_{k}}\delta_{i_{k}}/\epsilon

for
p\in S_{k}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Also, since
(S_{k})
are pairwise disjoint, we may assume
x\notin S_{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
By definition of
\mathcal{J}_{i_{k}}^{\epsilon}(f)
and
S_{k}\in\mathcal{J}_{i_{k}}^{\epsilon}(f)
,
there are
p_{k}^{-}\neq p_{k}^{+}\in S_{k}
such that

\|f(p_{k}^{-})-f(p_{k}^{+})\|_{Y}\geq\epsilon\eta_{i_{k}}\delta_{i_{k}}
.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define
x_{2k-1}:=p_{k}^{-}
,
x_{2k}:=p_{k}^{+}
,
and observe that
(x_{j})\subseteq X\setminus\{x\}
and
x_{j}\rightarrow x
.
Let
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to Y
be linear and note that, by triangle inequality and
\varphi(p_{k}^{-})=\varphi(p_{k}^{+})
,
there are
\sigma_{k}\in\{-1,+1\}
such that

(5.14)

\|f(p_{k}^{\sigma_{k}})-f(x)-T(\varphi(p_{k}^{\sigma_{k}})-\varphi(x))\|_{Y}\geq\epsilon\eta_{i_{k}}\delta_{i_{k}}/2

for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
(p_{k}^{\sigma_{k}})_{k}
is a subsequence of
(x_{j})
, we finally have from
Eq.

5.13
,
Eq.

5.14

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|f(x_{j})-f(x)-T(\varphi(x_{j})-\varphi(x))\|_{Y}}{d_{\eta}(x,x_{j})}

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|f(p_{k}^{\sigma_{k}})-f(x)-T(\varphi(p_{k}^{\sigma_{k}})-\varphi(x))\|_{Y}}{\eta_{i_{k}}\delta_{i_{k}}/\epsilon}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}.

∎

Proof of
5.6
.

We begin with the statement regarding points where
f
is differentiable.

Possibly replacing
Y
with the closed span of
f(X)
, we may assume
Y
to be separable.
Then, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the Banach space of linear maps
\mathbb{R}^{n}\to Y
is also separable.
Let
\{(U_{j},\varphi_{j}\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}})\}_{j}
be a Cheeger atlas compatible with the shortcuts and fix
\epsilon>0
.
Since
f
is in particular Lipschitz on
(X,d)
and
(X,d,\mu)
a
Y
-LDS,
we may cover
\mu
-almost all of
X\setminus\operatorname{Bad}(f)
with countably many Borel sets
E\subseteq X\setminus\operatorname{Bad}(f)

having the following properties.
There is
j
with
E\subseteq U_{j}
,
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}\to Y
linear, and
R_{1}>0
such that:

(i)

for
x\in E
and
y\in B_{X}(x,R_{1})
, it holds

\|f(y)-f(x)-T(\varphi_{j}(y)-\varphi_{j}(x))\|_{Y}\leq\epsilon d(x,y);

(ii)

E
is not porous w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
at any
x\in E
.

Set
C:=1+2a/b
, let
R>0
be as in
Lemma

5.9
with the current choices of parameters,
and assume further
R\leq R_{1}/C
.
We are now ready to prove differentiability.
Let
y\in B_{\eta}(x,R)\cap E
.
If
d_{\eta}(x,y)=d(x,y)
, then
R\leq R_{1}
and (
i
) give

(5.15)

\|f(y)-f(x)-T(\varphi_{j}(y)-\varphi_{j}(x))\|_{Y}\leq\epsilon d_{\eta}(x,y).

Suppose now
d_{\eta}(x,y)<d(x,y)
.
By
3.11
, we find
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}
such that

(5.16)

d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{+},y)\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y);

in particular,
d(x,p_{-}),d(y,p_{+})\leq CR\leq R_{1}
and
y,p_{-},p_{+}
are as in
Lemma

5.9
.
Thus, item (
i
) and
Eq.

5.10
give

(5.17)

\displaystyle\|f(y)-f(x)-T(\varphi_{j}(y)-\varphi_{j}(x))\|_{Y}

\displaystyle\leq\|f(y)-f(p_{+})-T(\varphi_{j}(y)-\varphi_{j}(p_{+}))\|_{Y}+\|f(p_{+})-f(p_{-})\|_{Y}

\displaystyle+\|f(p_{-})-f(x)-T(\varphi_{j}(p_{-})-\varphi_{j}(x))\|_{Y}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon d(y,p_{+})+\epsilon d_{\eta}(x,y)+\epsilon d(x,p_{-})

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon(C+1)d_{\eta}(x,y),

where we have also used
\varphi_{j}(p_{-})=\varphi_{j}(p_{+})
and
Eq.

5.16
.
Hence,
Eq.

5.15
and
Eq.

5.17
imply

\operatorname{Lip}_{\eta}((f-T\circ\varphi_{j})|_{E};x)\leq(C+1)\epsilon
for
x\in E
.
But then, by item (
ii
) and
Lemma

2.9
,
we have
\operatorname{Lip}_{\eta}(f-T\circ\varphi_{j};x)\leq(C+1)\epsilon
for every
x\in E
.
Since, for every
\epsilon>0
,
X\setminus\operatorname{Bad}(f)
is
\mu
-almost all covered by sets such as
E
, we have proven
that for every
\epsilon>0
,
j
, and
\mu
-a.e.
x\in U_{j}\setminus\operatorname{Bad}(f)
, there is a linear map
T_{x,\epsilon}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}\to Y

such that
\operatorname{Lip}_{\eta}(f-T_{x,\epsilon}\circ\varphi_{j};x)\leq\epsilon
.

Lemma

2.7
and

\operatorname{Lip}_{\eta}(\langle v,\varphi_{j}\rangle;x)\geq\operatorname{Lip}(\langle v,\varphi_{j}\rangle;x)>0

for
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}\setminus\{0\}
and
\mu
-a.e.
x\in U_{j}
conclude the proof
of differentiability; see
Lemma

2.5
.
Lastly, the differentials w.r.t. the two distances agree because they are unique
\mu
-a.e. and
d_{\eta}\leq d
.

We now consider points where
f
is not differentiable.

Let
\{(U_{j},\varphi_{j}\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}})\}_{j}
be a compatible atlas and
A\subseteq\operatorname{Bad}(f)
a set.
Let
N\subseteq X
be the set of porosity points of
A
and recall that
\mu(N)=0
by assumption.
By
Lemma

5.12
, for every
j
and
x\in A\cap U_{j}
, we have

\operatorname{Lip}_{\eta}(f-T\circ\varphi_{j};x)>0
for every linear
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}\to Y
.
But then, by
Lemma

2.9
, we have

\operatorname{Lip}_{\eta}((f-T\circ\varphi_{j})|_{A};x)>0

for every linear map
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{n_{j}}\to Y
and
x\in A\cap U_{j}\setminus N
.
∎

As mentioned in the introduciton, in general the set
\mathrm{Bad}(f)
may have positive
\mu
measure, as is demonstrated by the example in
10.15

and, for different targets, by

10.13
and
10.16
.
However, this is not possible if the space satisfies the following quantitative differentiation hypothesis.
Note that if it holds on the non-contracted space, then it holds also on the contracted one.

Corollary 5.18
.

Let
Y
be a Banach space and let
(X,d,\mu)
be an
s
-ADR
Y
-LDS with compatible shortcuts.
Suppose that, for any Lipschitz map
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
with bounded support,
it holds

\sum_{S\in\mathcal{J}}(\operatorname{diam}f(S))^{s}<\infty.

Then
\mu(\operatorname{Bad}(f))=0
for all Lipschitz
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
.
In particular,
(X,d_{\eta},\mu)
is a
Y
-LDS.

Proof.

Let
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
be Lipschitz.
Fix
x_{0}\in X
and, for
R>0
, let
f_{R}\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
be a Lipschitz function
with support in
B_{\eta}(x_{0},2R)
and which agrees with
f
on
B_{\eta}(x_{0},R)
.
Let
\epsilon>0
and observe that for
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\epsilon}(f_{R})
, we have

\mu(B_{\eta}(S,\eta_{i}\delta_{i}/\epsilon))\lesssim\epsilon^{-2s}(\operatorname{diam}f_{R}(S))^{s}
.
Hence, the hypothesis gives

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}^{\epsilon}(f_{R})}\mu(B_{\eta}(S,\eta_{i}\delta_{i}/\epsilon))\lesssim\epsilon^{-2s}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{J}}\left(\operatorname{diam}f_{R}(S)\right)^{s}<\infty,

which implies
\mu(\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f_{R}))=0
by the first Borel-Cantelli lemma.
By the definition of
\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f)
, it follows that
\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f)\cap B_{\eta}(x_{0},R/2)\subseteq\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f_{R})
,
and therefore
\mu(\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f)\cap B_{\eta}(x_{0},R/2))=0
for all
\epsilon,R>0
.
Thus, we have
\mu(\operatorname{Bad}(f))=0
for Lipschitz
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
,
and rest of the thesis follows from
5.7
,
Lemma

2.11
, and
Lemma

3.18
.
∎

6.
Laakso spaces

Laakso spaces were introduced in
[
Laa00
]
as examples of Ahlfors-David regular
1
-PI spaces whose Hausdorff dimension varies continuously in
(1,\infty)
.
We now describe the construction.

Let
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
M\geq 2
,
\theta\in(0,1/4]
, and
(N_{n})\subseteq 2\mathbb{N}
,
N_{n}\geq 4
, such that

(6.1)

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{N_{i}}\sim\theta^{n},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

It is convenient to metrise
[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
as follows.
For
x\neq y\in[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
, let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be the greatest integer such that

x(i)=y(i)
for
1\leq i<n
,
and set
d(x,y)=\theta^{n}
.

Set
\tilde{X}:=[0,1]\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
and endow it with the product topology (and metric).
Let
h\colon\tilde{X}\to[0,1]
denote the projection onto the first coordinate,
which we will call
height
.
For
i\in\mathbb{N}
, set

W_{i}^{h}:=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{i}\frac{t_{j}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{j}}\colon t_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},0\leq t_{j}<N_{j}\text{ for }1\leq j<i\text{ and }1\leq t_{i}<N_{i}\right\},

whose elements will be called
wormhole heights of level
i
.
We also set

(6.2)

W_{\leq i}^{h}:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{i}W_{j}^{h}=\left\{\frac{m}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\colon m\in\mathbb{N},0<m<N_{1}\cdots N_{i}\right\}.

We now define an equivalence relation on
\tilde{X}
.
Given
x,y\in\tilde{X}
, we write
x\sim y
if either
x=y
, or there is
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
h(x)=h(y)\in W_{i}^{h}
and

x,y
differ at most in the
i
-th digit, i.e.
x(j)=y(j)
for all
j\neq i
.
Let
X
denote the quotient space and
q\colon\tilde{X}\to X
the quotient map.

We call
wormholes of level
i
points
x\in X
for which
h(x)\in W^{h}_{i}
.
Note that for
x\in X
,
q^{-1}\{x\}
is a singleton unless
x
is a wormhole of some level
i\in\mathbb{N}
, in which case
\#q^{-1}\{x\}=M

and for
a\in[M]
there is
y\in q^{-1}\{x\}
with
y(i)=a
.
Hence, if
x\in X
is not a wormhole, we can talk about its digits, denoted by
(x(j))_{j}
.
Instead, if
x
is a wormhole of some level
i\in\mathbb{N}
, its digit of level
i
is not defined, but the ones of level
j\neq i
are, which we still denote as
x(j)
.
We then say that

x,y\in X

differ at the
i
-th digit
or
differ at the digit of level
i

if
\tilde{x}(i)\neq\tilde{y}(i)

for all representatives
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\in\tilde{X}
of
x,y
respectively.
In this case, we also write
x(i)\neq y(i)
.

We metrise
X
by setting

d(x,y):=\inf\{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\gamma):q\circ\gamma\text{ is a continuous curve from }x\text{ to }y\},

for
x,y\in X
.

Remark 6.3
.

The above distance can be informally described as follows.
Let
x,y\in X
and let

\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{N}
be the set of levels
j
for which
x(j)\neq y(j)
.
Any path from
x
to
y
has to change digits at each level in
\Delta
,
which can occur only by traveling through wormholes of the corresponding level.
Paths from
x
to
y
can be explicitly constructed as follows.
Let
I\subseteq[0,1]
be an interval containing
h(x),h(y)
and intersecting
W_{i}^{h}
for each
i\in\Delta
.
We can then travel
from
x
to the closest boundary point of
I
, then along
I
, and lastly to
y
, changing all necessary digits along the way;
the resulting path has length
2|I|-|h(x)-h(y)|
.
It turns out the there is always an optimal path of this form.

Proposition 6.4

(
[
Laa00
, Proposition 1.1]
)
.

Let
x,y\in X
and
\ell
the minimum among lengths of intervals containing
h(x),h(y)
, and all wormhole heights necessary to travel from
x
to
y
.
Then

d(x,y)=2\ell-|h(x)-h(y)|.

It is not difficult to verify that
d
induces the quotient topology on
X
,
q\colon\tilde{X}\to X
is David-Semmes regular,
and
h\colon X\to[0,1]
is open
[
Laa00
]
.

For
M,(N_{n})
, and
d
as above, we call
Laakso space
the metric space
(X,d)
and,
when we want to highlight the dependence on
M
and
(N_{n})
, we write

X(M;(N_{n}))
.

Remark 6.5
.

In
[
Laa00
]
, Laakso allows for a slightly larger family of sequences
(N_{n})
than us.
Hence, what we call Laakso spaces form a strict subset of the ones considered in
[
Laa00
]
.
Nonetheless, as will be clarified shortly, the spaces we consider can still achieve any Hausdorff dimension in
(1,\infty)
.

Laakso proves the following.

Theorem 6.6

(
[
Laa00
]
)
.

Let
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
be a Laakso space and
s\in(1,\infty)
such that

M\theta^{s-1}=1
, i.e.
s=1+\log M/\log(1/\theta)
.
Then
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
is a compact geodesic
s
-ADR
1
-PI space.

In particular, by
[
CK09
]
,
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
is a
Y
-LDS
for any Banach space
Y
with RNP.

The following lemma gives more precise information on the charts.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
be an
s
-ADR Laakso space.
Then the height function
h\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
is a global Cheeger chart, satisfying
\operatorname{Lip}(h;\cdot)=1
everywhere.

Proof.

The equality
\operatorname{Lip}(h;\cdot)=1
is clear.

One can prove directly that
h
is a chart following an argument analogous to

[
CPS25
]
.
For brevity, we explain how this follows from results in the literature.

From
[
Wea00
, 5.A]
,
the module of Weaver derivations on
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
has exactly one independent derivation.
Then, by
[
Sch16a
, Theorem 3.24]
,

(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
has exactly one independent Alberti representation and
hence, by
[
Bat15
, Theorem 6.6]
, the analytic dimension of
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
is
1
.

∎

To construct non-differentiable maps, it will be useful to consider the following sequence of approximations of
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
.

Definition 6.8
.

Set
\tilde{X}_{n}:=[0,1]\times[M]^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and let
\pi^{\infty}_{n}\colon\tilde{X}\to\tilde{X}_{n}
denote the coordinate projection map.
Similarly to
\sim
on
\tilde{X}
, we can define an equivalence relation on
\tilde{X}_{n}
, which then satisfies

x\sim y\text{ if and only if }\pi^{\infty}_{n}(x)\sim\pi^{\infty}_{n}(y)\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

We let
X_{n}
denote the quotient of
\tilde{X}_{n}
under the above equivalence relation and still denote with
q
the quotient map; this should cause no confusion.
The maps
\pi_{n}^{\infty}
induce maps
X\to X_{n}
, which we still denote with
\pi^{\infty}_{n}
.
We similarly have maps
\pi^{m}_{n}\colon\tilde{X}_{m}\to\tilde{X}_{n}
,
\pi^{m}_{n}\colon X_{m}\to X_{n}
for
0\leq n\leq m
.
We endow the spaces
X_{n}
with the quotient topology and define a distance similarly to
X
.

One can then recover
X
from
(X_{n})_{n}
in several ways.
Indeed, it is then not difficult to verify that
X
corresponds to the inverse limit of the inverse system given by
(X_{n})_{n}
and
(\pi^{n+1}_{n})_{n}
,
and that
(X_{n})_{n}
Gromov-Hausdorff converges to
X
; see e.g.
[
CK15
]
.

6.1.
Laakso spaces as metric graphs

Let
G=(V,E)
be a connected (abstract) graph,
\ell>0
, and endow it with its length distance
d_{G}
,
scaled such that
d_{G}(u,v)=\ell
for each edge
\{u,v\}\in E
.
We denote with
X_{G}
the metric space obtained by attaching an isometric copy of
[0,\ell]
to each pair of
neighbouring vertices.
We can then identify
V
with an isometric copy in
X_{G}
and each
e\in E
with the copy of
[0,\ell]
in
X_{G}
joining
\partial e
.
With these identifications, we call
(X_{G},V,E)
the
metic graph
associated to
G
.

The following will be useful in the construction of non-differentiable functions. We omit the proof.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
(X_{G},V,E)
be a metric graph associated to a connected graph
G=(V,E)
,
Y
a Banach space, and
f\colon V\to Y
a function.
Then there is a unique extension
F\colon X_{G}\to Y
of
f
which is affine linear on each edge of
X_{G}
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{LIP}(F)=\operatorname{LIP}(f)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We now define a graph
G_{n}=(V,E)
for which
X_{n}=X_{G_{n}}
isometrically.
The most natural choice for
G_{n}
would be a multigraph, but since we prefer working with graphs, our choice of
G_{n}
will be a topological minor of the natural multigraph.
For
1\leq l\leq n
, set

V_{l}:=W_{l}^{h}\times\left([M]^{l-1}\times\{0\}\times[M]^{n-l}\right)

and

V:=\bigcup_{l=1}^{n}V_{l}\cup\left(\left\{\frac{m}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n+1}}\colon m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},0\leq m\leq N_{1}\cdots N_{n+1}\right\}\setminus W^{h}_{\leq n}\right)\times[M]^{n}.

In the definition of
V_{l}
, the digit
0
should be considered simply as a ‘symbol’ outside the ‘alphabet’
[M]
.
For
v,v^{\prime}\in V
, we say that they adjacent if
|h(v)-h(v^{\prime})|=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n+1}}

and
v(j)=v^{\prime}(j)
for all
1\leq j\leq n
for which
v(j),v^{\prime}(j)\neq 0
.
This adjacency relation defines an edge set
E
and we set
G_{n}=(V,E)
.
It is then not difficult to verify that, taking
\ell=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n+1}}
, we have
X_{n}=X_{G_{n}}
isometrically.

6.2.
Cubical covers of Laakso spaces

We now define a family of covers of a Laakso space.

Definition 6.10
.

Let
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
be a Laakso space, fixed for the rest of the subsection.
For
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
define

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\{[0,1]\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{i}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left[\frac{m}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}},\frac{m+1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\right]\colon m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},0\leq m<N_{1}\cdots N_{i}\right\}.

Further, for
i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
,
a\in[M]^{j}
,
define

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{I,a}

\displaystyle:=I\times\{a\}\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}},

\displaystyle Q_{I,a}

\displaystyle:=q(I\times\{a\}\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{i}

\displaystyle:=\{Q_{I,a}\colon I\in\mathcal{I}_{i},a\in[M]^{i}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{i},

where we interpret
I\times\{a\}\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}}=I\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
if
j=0
.

Recall that given two sets
\Omega,Z
and a function
f\colon\Omega\to Z
, a set
E\subseteq\Omega
is

f
-saturated
if
f^{-1}(f(E))=E
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
j\leq i
and
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
,
a\in[M]^{j}
.
Then
\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a}
is
q
-saturated
and
\operatorname{Int}Q_{I,a}=q(\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a})
.

Proof.

We first prove that
\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a}
is
q
-saturated.
We may suppose
i\geq 1
, for otherwise
\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a}=\operatorname{Int}\tilde{X}=\tilde{X}
, which is
q
-saturated.
Let
J
denote the interior of
I
as a subset of
[0,1]
.
Then
\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a}=J\times\{a\}\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}}

and
J
does not intersect
W_{\leq i}^{h}
(see
Eq.

6.2
).
Thus, if
x\in\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a}
and
y\in q^{-1}\{q(x)\}
,
then
h(y)=h(x)\in J
and

y(k)=x(k)
for
k>i\geq j
,
proving that
\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a}
is
q
-saturated.

Being
q
a quotient map (in the topological sense),
q(\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a})
is open in
X
and so

q(\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a})\subseteq\operatorname{Int}Q_{I,a}
.
For the reverse inclusion, let
V\subseteq Q_{I,a}
be open.
Then
h(V)
is open in
[0,1]
and contained in
I
.
Hence,
h(V)\subseteq J
and
V
does not contain wormholes of level
>j
,
implying
q^{-1}(V)\subseteq\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a}
.
∎

Saturated sets have the following useful properties.
We include proofs for completeness.

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\Omega,Z
be sets,
f\colon\Omega\to Z
a function,
and suppose
E\subseteq\Omega
is
f
-saturated.
Then

f(E\cap A)=f(E)\cap f(A)

and
f(A\setminus E)=f(A)\setminus f(E)

for all
A\subseteq\Omega
.

Proof.

The inclusion

f(E\cap A)\subseteq f(E)\cap f(A)
is clear.
Let
z\in f(E)\cap f(A)
and
x\in A
with
f(x)=z
.
Then
x\in f^{-1}\{z\}\subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))=E
, proving
z\in f(E\cap A)

and hence
f(E\cap A)=f(E)\cap f(A)
.

The inclusion
f(A)\setminus f(E)\subseteq f(A\setminus E)
is clear.
Let
z\in f(A\setminus E)
and pick
x\in A\setminus E
such that
f(x)=z
.
If
z\in f(E)
, then
x\in f^{-1}\{z\}\subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))=E
, a contradiction.
Thus, we have
f(A\setminus E)\subseteq f(A)\setminus f(E)
, concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
j\leq i
and
I,I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
,
a,a^{\prime}\in[M]^{j}
.
Then
\partial Q_{I,a}=q(\partial\tilde{Q}_{I,a})
and

Q_{I,a}\cap Q_{I^{\prime},a^{\prime}}=\partial Q_{I,a}\cap\partial Q_{I^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
,
whenever
(I,a)\neq(I^{\prime},a^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We only need to apply
Lemmas

6.11
and
6.12
.
We have

\displaystyle\partial Q_{I,a}

\displaystyle=q(\tilde{Q}_{I,a})\setminus q(\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a})=q(\tilde{Q}_{I,a}\setminus\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I,a})=q(\partial\tilde{Q}_{I,a}),

\displaystyle Q_{I,a}\cap\operatorname{Int}Q_{I^{\prime},a^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=q(\tilde{Q}_{I,a})\cap q(\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I^{\prime},a^{\prime}})=q(\tilde{Q}_{I,a}\cap\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I^{\prime},a^{\prime}})=\varnothing,

where in the second line we have used

\tilde{Q}_{I,a}\cap\operatorname{Int}\tilde{Q}_{I^{\prime},a^{\prime}}=\varnothing
for
(I,a)\neq(I^{\prime},a^{\prime})
.
∎

To conclude, we gather the most relevant properties of
(\mathcal{Q}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
be an
s
-ADR Laakso space and let
(\mathcal{Q}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

be as in
6.10
.
For
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is a finite compact cover of
X
satisfying:

(i)

\mathcal{H}^{s}(\partial Q)=0
for
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
;

(ii)

Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\partial Q\cap\partial Q^{\prime}
for
Q\neq Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
;

(iii)

for
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
and
j>i
it holds

Q_{0}=\bigcup\{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}\colon Q\subseteq Q_{0}\}.

Moreover, there is
C=C(X)\geq 1
such that
for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
it holds

(6.15)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\theta^{i}

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{diam}Q\leq C\theta^{i}

\displaystyle C^{-1}\theta^{is}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{s}(Q)\leq C\theta^{is}

Here
\theta
is as in
Eq.

6.1
.

Proof.

To see that
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is compact, recall that
q
is continuous.
Item (
ii
) is the content of
Lemma

6.13
,
while (
iii
) follows from the definition of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.
Let
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
Q=Q_{I,a}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.
Since
q
is David-Semmes regular, by
Lemma

6.13
we have (see
Eq.

3.16
)

(6.16)

\mathcal{H}^{s}_{X}(\partial Q)\sim q_{\#}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\tilde{X}}(q(\partial\tilde{Q}_{I,a}))=\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\tilde{X}}(q^{-1}(q(\partial\tilde{Q}_{I,a})).

Let
J
denote the interior of
I
as a subset of
[0,1]
.
Then
\partial\tilde{Q}_{I,a}=(I\setminus J)\times\{a\}\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
,

\#(I\setminus J)\leq 2
, and
q^{-1}(q(\partial\tilde{Q}_{I,a}))\subseteq(I\setminus J)\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Since
\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\tilde{X}}((J\setminus I)\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}})=0
,
Eq.

6.16
concludes the proof of (
i
).

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.
Since
\mathcal{Q}_{0}=\{X\}
, we may assume
i\in\mathbb{N}
and take
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
,
a\in[M]^{i}
such that
Q=Q_{I,a}
.
From
6.4
, it is clear that
\operatorname{diam}Q_{I,a}\sim\theta^{i}
.
Since
\mathcal{H}^{s}
is
s
-ADR (see
6.6
), it follows
\mathcal{H}^{s}(Q)\lesssim\theta^{is}
.
From the choice of distance on
[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
and
Eq.

6.1
, it is not difficult to see that

\tilde{Q}_{I,a}
contains a ball of
\tilde{X}
of radius
\sim\theta^{i}
.
Since
q\colon\tilde{X}\to X
is DS-regular, we finally have

\mathcal{H}^{s}_{X}(Q)\sim q_{\#}\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\tilde{X}}(Q)\geq\mathcal{H}^{s}_{\tilde{X}}(\tilde{Q}_{I,a})\gtrsim\theta^{is}.

∎

6.3.
Shortcuts in Laakso spaces

In this subsection we show that Laakso spaces have shortcuts, see
6.25
.

Let
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
be a Laakso space, fixed for the subsection.

Definition 6.17
.

For
i\in\mathbb{N}
, set

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{i}\frac{t_{j}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{j}}+\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\colon t_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},0\leq t_{j}<N_{j}\text{ for }1\leq j<i\text{ and }1\leq t_{i}<N_{i}-1\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{a+b}{2}\colon[a,b]\in\mathcal{I}_{i}\text{ and }a,b\in W_{i}^{h}\right\}.

When we wish to emphasise the dependence on
N_{1},\dots,N_{i}
in
\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}
, we then write

(6.18)

\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}=\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{i}).

Since
N_{i+1}
is even,
\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}\subseteq W_{i+1}^{h}
.
We define the following set of
shortcuts of level
i

\mathcal{J}_{i}:=\left\{q(\{t\}\times\{a\}\times[M]\times\{1\}^{\mathbb{N}})\colon t\in\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}\text{ and }a\in[M]^{i-1}\right\},

where, for
i=1
, we interpret

\{t\}\times\{a\}\times[M]\times\{1\}^{\mathbb{N}}=\{t\}\times[M]\times\{1\}^{\mathbb{N}}.

Also define

\delta_{i}:=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}.

Finally, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider
X_{n}
defined as in
Section

6.1
.
We also define
(\mathcal{J}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
similarly as above also in
X_{n}
.

We now proceed to prove that the sets
\mathcal{J}_{i}
defined above are indeed shortcuts in the Laakso space
X
.

We will need the following fact, often used without mention.
Let
N_{1},\dots,N_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
0\leq m<N_{1}\cdots N_{i}
, and set
t:=\tfrac{m}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
.
Then, there are unique
t_{1},\dots,t_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
0\leq t_{j}<N_{j}
, such that

t=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\frac{t_{j}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{j}}.

Lemma 6.19
.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
, and let

t\in(0,1)
,
a\in[M]^{i-1}
be such that
S=q(\{t\}\times\{a\}\times[M]\times\{1\}^{\mathbb{N}})
.
Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
be the interval having
t
as midpoint
and suppose
x\notin\operatorname{Int}Q_{I,a}
.

Then
d(x,z)=d(x,S)\geq\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
for
z\in S
.
In particular,

d(x,y)\leq d(x,z)+d(w,y)

for
x,y\notin\operatorname{Int}Q_{I,a}
and
z,w\in S
.

Proof.

If
h(x)\notin\operatorname{Int}I
, then any geodesic from
x
to
z\in S
has to cross
\partial I\subseteq W_{i}^{h}

and therefore meet a wormhole of level
i
.
At such intersection point we can modify the
i
-th digit and obtain a geodesic from
x
to
w
,
for any
w\in S
.
Hence,
d(x,z)=d(x,S)
for
z\in S
.
Since
h
is
1
-Lipschitz,
d(x,S)\geq|h(x)-t|=\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
.

Suppose now
x\in Q_{I,b}
for some
b\in[M]^{i-1}
,
b\neq a
,
and note that
I
does not contain wormholes heights of level
\leq i-1
.
Since
b\neq a
,
there is
1\leq j\leq i-1
such that
x(j)=b(j)\neq a(j)
.
Again,
since
W_{j}^{h}\cap I=\varnothing
, any geodesic from
x
to
z\in S
has to cross
\partial I

and therefore meet a wormhole of level
i
.
It follows that
d(x,S)=d(x,z)
and
d(x,S)\geq\frac{3}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
, and let

t\in(0,1)
,
a\in[M]^{i-1}
be such that
S=q(\{t\}\times\{a\}\times[M]\times\{1\}^{\mathbb{N}})
.
Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
be the interval having
t
as midpoint.
Then
\operatorname{Int}Q_{I,a}=U_{X}(S,\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}})
.

Proof.

Lemma

6.19
gives
U_{X}(S,\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}})\subseteq\operatorname{Int}Q_{I,a}
.
Fix
x\in\operatorname{Int}Q_{I,a}
and let

p\in S
be such that
x(j)=p(j)
for
1\leq j\leq i
.
Since
\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}\subseteq W_{i+1}^{h}
, we need to change only digits of level
j\geq i+2
.
If the interval with endpoints
\{h(x),t\}
intersects
W_{i+2}^{h}
,
then
d(x,p)=|h(x)-t|
, because
t\in W_{i+1}^{h}
and between wormhole heights of levels
l_{1},l_{2}

there are wormholes heights of level
l

for each
l\geq\max(l_{1},l_{2})
.
Suppose now there are no wormholes heights of level
i+2
between
t
and
h(x)
.
Then
|h(x)-t|<\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i+2}}
.
Let
y\in X
be such that
h(y)\in W_{i+2}^{h}
,
|h(y)-h(x)|=d(h(x),W_{i+2}^{h})
, and
y(j)=x(j)
for all
j
.
Then
d(y,p)=|h(y)-t|=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i+2}}
,
d(x,p)=d(x,y)+d(y,p)
,
and
d(x,y)=|h(x)-h(y)|=|h(y)-t|-|t-h(x)|
.
Hence,

d(x,p)\leq 2|h(y)-t|=\frac{2}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i+2}}<\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}},

because
N_{i+1}N_{i+2}>4
.
∎

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
i\leq j
and
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
,
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{j}
be distinct.
Then
d(S,S^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{j}}
.

Proof.

Let
z\in S
,
z^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
, and
t=h(z)
,
t^{\prime}=h(z^{\prime})
.
If
t\neq t^{\prime}
, then

d(z,z^{\prime})\geq|t-t^{\prime}|\geq\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{j}}.

Suppose now
t=t^{\prime}
. Since
(\mathcal{J}_{k}^{h})_{k}
is pairwise disjoint,
in particular we have
i=j\geq 2
.
Let
a\neq a^{\prime}\in[M]^{j-1}
such that
S,S^{\prime}
are determined by
(t,a)
and
(t,a^{\prime})
respectively,
and let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{j}
be the interval having
t
as midpoint.
Let
1\leq k\leq j-1
be such that
a(k)\neq a^{\prime}(k)
and hence
z(k)\neq z^{\prime}(k)
.
Since there are no wormholes heights of level
k
in
I
, we have

d(z,z^{\prime})\geq 2d(t,W_{k}^{h})=\frac{3}{N_{1}\cdots N_{j}}.

∎

Lemma 6.22
.

For
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
z\neq w\in S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
it holds

d(z,w)=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
.

Proof.

The points
z,w
differ exactly in the
i
-th digit and
d(h(z),W_{i}^{h})=d(h(w),W_{i}^{h})=\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in W_{i}^{h}
. Then there is
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
such that
t\in\partial I\subseteq W_{i}^{h}
.

Proof.

We can write
t=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\frac{t_{j}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{j}}
with
t_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
0\leq t_{j}<N_{j}
for
1\leq j<i
and
1\leq t_{i}\leq N_{i}-1
.
If
t_{i}\leq N_{i}-2
, then
t^{\prime}:=t+\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\in W_{i}^{h}
and
[t,t^{\prime}]\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
.
If
t_{i}=N_{i}-1>1
, then
t^{\prime}:=t-\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\in W_{i}^{h}
and
[t^{\prime},t]\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 6.24
.

For
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in X
, it holds
d(x,\cup\mathcal{J}_{i})\leq\frac{3}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
.

Proof.

Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
be such that
h(x)\in I
.
Since
\partial I\cap W_{i}^{h}\neq\varnothing
, by
Lemma

6.23
there is
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{i}

with
\partial I^{\prime}\subseteq W_{i}^{h}
and
\partial I\cap\partial I^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
.
Let
z\in X
be such that
h(z)\in\partial I\cap\partial I^{\prime}
and
z(j)=x(j)
for all
j
.
Then

d(x,\cup\mathcal{J}_{i})\leq d(x,z)+d(z,\cup\mathcal{J}_{i})\leq\frac{3}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}.

∎

Proposition 6.25
.

Let
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
be an
s
-ADR Laakso space.
Then
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i\geq 1}
, defined as in
6.17
,
are shortcuts compatible with
the differentiable structure of
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
.

More precisely, it satisfies the conditions of
3.1

with parameters
a=a_{0}=1
,
b=1/2
, and
M=M
,
and
\operatorname{diam}h(S)=0
for
S\in\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

This follows combining the previous lemmas.
The first condition of
3.1
is given by
Lemma

6.22
,
the second by
Lemma

6.21
, the third by
Lemma

6.19
and
Lemma

6.20
,
the fourth by the definition of
(\mathcal{J}_{i})_{i}
, and the last by
Lemma

6.24
(and
M\geq 2
).

It is a PI space
[
Laa00
]
and hence
a
Y
-LDS for any Banach space
Y
with RNP
by
[
CK09
]
.
Compatibility of the shortcuts with the differentiable structure follows by definition of
(\mathcal{J}_{i})_{i}

and
Lemma

6.7
.
∎

7.
Harmonic approximation on LDS of analytic dimension 1

The main result of this subsection is
7.8
.
Under suitable assumptions, it decomposes a Lipschitz map
f\colon X\to Y
into a sum of Lipschitz
maps
(F_{i+1}-F_{i})
with control of the total energy of the sequence (see
7.2
), where
F_{i}
agrees with
f
on a ‘grid’ of the space.
This will be used later, in
8.3
, to prove that
shortcut Laakso spaces are
Y
-LDS for some Banach spaces
Y
.

The proof of
7.8
is inspired
by Pisier’s martingale inequality
[
Pis16
, Theorem 10.6]
,
where we replace Jensen’s inequality with a local minimality condition.

Piecewise harmonic approximations of
\ell_{2}
valued maps appear in
[
Sch16b
]
.
Our construction differs by building the harmonic approximations in an LDS, rather than a cube complex, and so we only have access to the Cheeger derivative.
On the other hand, our construction does not rely on piecewise Euclidean approximations of a metric space,
nor Euler-Lagrange equations, and holds for general (superreflexive) Banach space targets.
We also do not need to study the
regularity of minimisers, nor find covers with regular boundaries (in the potential-theoretic sense).

Recall that a Banach space
Y
is
q
-uniformly convex,
2\leq q<\infty
, if its
modulus of uniform convexity
\delta_{Y}(\cdot)
satisfies
\delta_{Y}(\epsilon)\geq c\epsilon^{q}
for some
c>0

and all
\epsilon\in(0,2]
.
Equivalently, there is
K>0
such that the inequality

(7.1)

\left\|\frac{x+y}{2}\right\|_{Y}^{q}+\frac{1}{K^{q}}\left\|\frac{x-y}{2}\right\|_{Y}^{q}\leq\frac{\|x\|_{Y}^{q}}{2}+\frac{\|y\|_{Y}^{q}}{2}

holds for
x,y\in Y
; see e.g.
[
Pis16
, Proposition 10.31]
.
We let
K_{q}(Y)
denote the least
K
as in
Eq.

7.1
.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
(X,d,\mu)
is a metric measure space,
1\leq q<\infty
, and
Y
a Banach space.
For
f\colon X\to Y
Lipschitz and
Q\subseteq X

\mu
-measurable, we define the
q
-energy
of
f
on
Q
as

E_{q}(f,Q):=\int_{Q}\operatorname{Lip}(f;x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
Y
be a
q
-uniformly convex Banach space,
(X,d,\mu)
a
Y
-LDS,
and
Q\subseteq X
a
\mu
-measurable set of analytic dimension
1
.
Then, for any
u,v\colon X\to Y
Lipschitz, we have

2E_{q}\left(\tfrac{u+v}{2},Q\right)+2K_{q}(Y)^{-q}E_{q}\left(\tfrac{u-v}{2},Q\right)\leq E_{q}(u,Q)+E_{q}(v,Q).

Proof.

By definition of analytic dimension,
there are charts
(U_{i},\varphi_{i}\colon X\to\mathbb{R})
such that

U_{i}\subseteq Q
and
\mu(Q\setminus\bigcup_{i}U_{i})=0
.
Since
(U_{i},\varphi_{i})
is
1
-dimensional, we may identify
\varphi_{i}
-differentials with elements of
Y
,
so that
\operatorname{Lip}(f;x)=\|d_{x}f\|_{Y}\operatorname{Lip}(\varphi_{i};x)
for any
f\colon X\to Y
which is
\varphi_{i}
-differentiable at
x\in U_{i}
.
Hence,
Eq.

7.1
implies

2\operatorname{Lip}\left(\tfrac{u+v}{2};x\right)^{q}+2K_{q}(Y)^{-q}\operatorname{Lip}\left(\tfrac{u-v}{2};x\right)^{q}\leq\operatorname{Lip}(u;x)^{q}+\operatorname{Lip}(v;x)^{q}

at
\mu
-a.e.
x\in Q
.
Integrating over
Q
concludes the proof.
∎

Definition 7.4
.

Let
(X,d,\mu)
be a metric measure space,
1\leq q<\infty
, and
Y
a Banach space.
For
f\colon X\to Y
Lipschitz and
Q\subseteq X

\mu
-measurable, we define the following sets of admissible functions and minimisers, respectively,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(f,Q)

\displaystyle:=\{u\colon\overline{Q}\to Y\colon\operatorname{LIP}(u)\leq\operatorname{LIP}(f),u|_{\partial Q}=f|_{\partial Q}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)

\displaystyle:=\{u\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q)\colon E_{q}(u,Q)=\inf_{v\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q)}E_{q}(v,Q)\}.

Both
\mathcal{A}(f,Q)
and
\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
are convex subsets of
\operatorname{LIP}(X;Y)
and
\mathcal{A}(f,Q)
is never empty,
since
f|_{\overline{Q}}\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q)
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
Y
be a
q
-uniformly convex Banach space,

(X,d,\mu)
a
Y
-LDS,

Q\subseteq X
a
\mu
-measurable set,
and
f\colon X\to Y
a Lipschitz map.

Then
\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
is non-empty.
Moreover, if
Q
has analytic dimension
1
,
the elements
u\in\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
are characterised as those
u\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q)
satisfying

(7.6)

E_{q}(u,Q)+2(2K_{q}(V))^{-q}E_{q}(u-v,Q)\leq E_{q}(v,Q),\qquad v\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q).

Proof.

Proving existence requires a simple application of the direct method of calculus of variations.
If
\partial Q=\varnothing
, any constant function is admissible and a minimiser, hence
\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)\neq\varnothing
.
Suppose
\partial Q\neq\varnothing
and let
(u_{j})
be a minimising sequence.
Since
(u_{j})
is equiLipschitz and bounded at a point, it is pointwise bounded on
\overline{Q}
.
Using reflexivity of
Y
(see e.g.
[
Pis16
, Theorem 10.3]
), Mazur’s lemma,
and arguing as in Arzelà-Ascoli’s theorem, we may suppose
(u_{j})
converges pointwise to a function
u\colon\overline{Q}\to Y
,
which then belongs to
\mathcal{A}(f,Q)
.
Moreover, by
Lemma

2.14
, we have

E_{q}(u,Q)\leq\lim_{j}E_{q}(u_{j},Q)=\inf_{v\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q)}E_{q}(v,Q)
, proving
u\in\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
.

It remains to prove the variational characterisation of minimisers.
Let
u\in\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
,
v\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q)
and observe that
(u+v)/2\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q)
.
We may assume
E_{q}(v,Q)<\infty
, so that
E_{q}(u,Q)\leq E_{q}(\tfrac{u+v}{2},Q)<\infty
.
Then
Lemma

7.3
and minimality of
u
give

\displaystyle 2K_{q}(Y)^{-q}E_{q}\left(\tfrac{u-v}{2},Q\right)

\displaystyle\leq E_{q}(u,Q)+E_{q}(v,Q)-2E_{q}\left(\tfrac{u+v}{2},Q\right)

\displaystyle\leq E_{q}(v,Q)-E_{q}(u,Q).

Lastly, if
u\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q)
satisfies
Eq.

7.6
, non-negativity of
E_{q}(\cdot,Q)
implies
u\in\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
.
∎

The following is an immediate application of
Lemma

2.25
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
Y
be a Banach space,
1\leq q<\infty
,
and let
(X,d,\mu)
be a metric measure space.
