Suppose
(X,d)
is a length space
and let

\mathcal{Q}
be a locally finite closed cover of
X
satisfying

Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\partial Q\cap\partial Q^{\prime}
for
Q\neq Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Then, for any Lipschitz
f\colon X\to Y
and
(u_{Q})_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}
with
u_{Q}\in\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
,
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
,
there is a unique function
F\colon X\to Y

satisfying
F|_{Q}=u_{Q}
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{LIP}(F)\leq\operatorname{LIP}(f)
.

Note that a locally finite cover
\mathcal{Q}
of
X
is necessarily countable.
Indeed, there is an open cover
\mathcal{U}
of
X
of sets intersecting only finitely many elements of
\mathcal{Q}
.
Since
X
is separable, it has the Lindelöf property, and so there is a countable subcover
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.
Then
\mathcal{Q}\setminus\{\varnothing\}=\bigcup_{U\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}\{Q\in\mathcal{Q}\colon Q\cap U\neq\varnothing\}
is a countable union of finite sets.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
Y
be a
q
-uniformly convex Banach space,
(X,d,\mu)
a
Y
-LDS
of analytic dimension
1
,
and suppose
(X,d)
is a length space.

For
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
be a locally finite closed cover of
X
satisfying:

(i)

\mu(\partial Q)=0
for
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
;

(ii)

Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\partial Q\cap\partial Q^{\prime}
for
Q\neq Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
;

(iii)

for
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
and
j>i
it holds

Q_{0}=\bigcup\{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}\colon Q\subseteq Q_{0}\}.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be
L
-Lipschitz and, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let

F_{i}\colon X\to Y
be such that
F_{i}|_{Q}\in\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
for
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.
Then, for
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i_{0}}
, we have

\sum_{i\geq i_{0}}E_{q}(F_{i}-F_{i+1},Q_{0})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\left(2K_{q}(Y)L\right)^{q}\mu(Q_{0}).

Proof.

Set
c:=2(2K_{q}(Y))^{-q}
and assume w.l.o.g.
\mu(Q_{0})<\infty
.
Observe that
F_{i+1}|_{Q}\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q)
for
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.
Indeed,

\partial Q\subseteq\bigcup\{\partial Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}\colon Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q\}

implies
F_{i+1}|_{\partial Q}=f|_{\partial Q}
,
while
Lemma

7.7
gives
\operatorname{LIP}(F_{i+1})\leq\operatorname{LIP}(f)
.
Then, for
i\geq i_{0}
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
with
Q\subseteq Q_{0}
, we have
(by
Lemma

7.5
)

\displaystyle cE_{q}(F_{i}-F_{i+1},Q)\leq E_{q}(F_{i+1},Q)-E_{q}(F_{i},Q),

where we have also used
\mu(Q)\leq\mu(Q_{0})<\infty
.
Hence, for
i\geq i_{0}
,

(7.9)

\displaystyle cE_{q}(F_{i}-F_{i+1},Q_{0})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}\colon\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}cE_{q}(F_{i}-F_{i+1},Q)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}\colon\\
Q\subseteq Q_{0}\end{subarray}}E_{q}(F_{i+1},Q)-E_{q}(F_{i},Q)

\displaystyle=E_{q}(F_{i+1},Q_{0})-E_{q}(F_{i},Q_{0}),

because
the collection

\{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}\colon Q\subseteq Q_{0}\}
is an essentially disjoint countable cover of
Q_{0}
.
Finally, the thesis follows from

\sum_{i\geq i_{0}}cE_{q}(F_{i}-F_{i+1},Q_{0})\leq\sum_{i\geq i_{0}}E_{q}(F_{i+1},Q_{0})-E_{q}(F_{i},Q_{0})\leq\lim_{i}E_{q}(F_{i},Q_{0})\leq L^{q}\mu(Q_{0}).

∎

Remark 7.10
.

Note that the proof of
7.8
relies on the exact cancellation of terms in the telescopic sum
Eq.

7.9
.
This is possible due to the precise constants appearing in
Lemmas

7.5
and
7.3
,
which is where we use the assumption that the space has analytic dimension 1.

8.
Quantitative differentiation on Laakso spaces

In this section we first apply our harmonic decomposition given in
7.8

to the cubical cover of Laakso spaces defined in
6.10
.
Because of the symmetry of Laakso spaces, the approximation satisfies an additional property
(see
Lemma

8.2
, item (
ii
)),
which is crucial to examine the behaviour of Lipschitz functions on Laakso spaces.
In particular,
8.3
establishes the quantitative differentiation hypothesis of
5.18
for Laakso spaces.
This will give the second item of

1.1
in
9.2
.

Let
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
be a Laakso space.
For
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\sigma_{i}
be a permutation of
[M]
.
Then the map
\tilde{X}\to\tilde{X}
which applies
\sigma_{i}
to the
i
-th digit, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, descends
to an isometry
X\to X
.
We now expand upon this fact.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
be an
s
-ADR Laakso space.
Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
with
\partial I\subseteq W_{i}^{h}
, and
a,b\in[M]^{i}

with
a(j)=b(j)
for
1\leq j<i
(any
a,b\in[M]
if
i=1
).
Let
T\colon X\to X
denote the isometry induced by the transposition
(a(i),b(i))
acting on the
i
-th digit.
Then, for every Banach space
Y
,
1\leq q<\infty
,
f\colon X\to Y
Lipschitz, and
u\colon Q_{I,a}\to Y

there hold:

•

u\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q_{I,a})
if and only if
u\circ T\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q_{I,b})
;

•

E_{q}(u,Q_{I,a})=E_{q}(u\circ T,Q_{I,b})
, provided
u
(equivalently,
u\circ T
) is Lipschitz.

Proof.

We first focus on the first point.
The condition on the Lipschitz constant in the definition of
\mathcal{A}(f,Q_{I,a})

is preserved by
T
because it is an isometry.
For the boundary conditions,
by
Lemma

6.13
, we know that
\partial Q_{I,b}=q(\partial I\times\{b\}\times[M])

and for

t\in\partial I
,
w\in[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

u\circ T\circ q(t,b,w)=u\circ q(t,a,w)=f\circ q(t,a,w)=f\circ q(t,b,w),

where we have used the definition of
T
,
u\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q_{I,a})
, and
q(t,a,w)=q(t,b,w)
.
This shows that
u\circ T\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q_{I,b})
whenever
u\in\mathcal{A}(f,Q_{I,a})
; the reverse statement holds by symmetry of the claim.
For the last point,
using the fact that
T
is an isometry, we have

\operatorname{Lip}(u\circ T;x)=\operatorname{Lip}(u;T(x))
for
\mathcal{H}^{s}
-a.e.
x\in Q_{I,a}
and so

E_{q}(u\circ T,Q_{I,b})=\int_{T^{-1}(Q_{I,a})}\operatorname{Lip}(u;T(x))^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{s}(x)=\int_{Q_{I,a}}\operatorname{Lip}(u;x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}(T_{\#}\mathcal{H}^{s})(x),

concluding the proof (because
T_{\#}\mathcal{H}^{s}=\mathcal{H}^{s}
).
∎

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
be an
s
-ADR Laakso space,

Y
a
q
-uniformly convex Banach space, and
f\colon X\to Y
an
L
-Lipschitz map.
Then, for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there are
L
-Lipschitz maps
F_{i}\colon X\to Y
satisfying:

(i)

F_{i}|_{Q}\in\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
for
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
;

(ii)

if
i\geq 1
,
for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
with
\partial I\subseteq W_{i}^{h}

and
a,b\in[M]^{i}
with
a(j)=b(j)
,
1\leq j<i
,
(any
a,b\in[M]
if
i=1
)
we have

F_{i}\circ q(t,a,w)=F_{i}\circ q(t,b,w),

for
t\in I
and
w\in[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
;

(iii)

\sum_{i\geq i_{0}}E_{q}(F_{i+1}-F_{i},Q_{0})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(2K_{q}(Y)L)^{q}\mathcal{H}^{s}(Q_{0})
for
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i_{0}}
.

Proof.

By
Lemmas

6.14
and
7.8
,
item (
iii
) is implied by (
i
)
and it is then enough to construct
(F_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfying (
i
)
and (
ii
).
By
Lemma

7.5
, for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}

the set
\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
is non-empty,
while, by
Lemma

7.7
, for any collection
(u_{Q})_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}}
with
u_{Q}\in\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)

there is a unique
L
-Lipschitz map
F_{i}\colon X\to Y
with
F_{i}|_{Q}=u_{Q}
for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
.

Let
F_{0}
be defined as above, for an arbitrary choice of
(u_{Q})_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}}=(u_{X})

(e.g.
F_{0}:=0
)
and let
i\geq 1
.
By
Lemma

8.1
, there is a collection

(u_{Q})_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}}
with
u_{Q}\in\mathcal{E}_{q}(f,Q)
and,
if
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
and
a,b\in[M]^{i}
are as in (
ii
),
we have for
Q=Q_{I,a},Q^{\prime}=Q_{I,b}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}

u_{Q}\circ q(t,a,w)=u_{Q^{\prime}}\circ q(t,b,w),

for
t\in I
and
w\in[M]^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
F_{i}\colon X\to Y
be the
L
-Lipschitz map given by
Lemma

7.7
with this collection
(u_{Q})_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}}
.
It is clear that it satisfies (
i
) and (
ii
).
∎

Theorem 8.3
.

Suppose
2\leq q<s
.
Let
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
be an
s
-ADR Laakso space and
Y
a
q
-uniformly convex Banach space.
Then, there is a constant
C=C(X,q)>0
such that for
any
L
-Lipschitz function
f\colon(X,d)\to Y
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
we have

(8.4)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{J}:\\
S\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}\left(\operatorname{diam}f(S)\right)^{s}\leq CK_{q}(Y)^{q}L^{s}\mathcal{H}^{s}(Q).

Here
\mathcal{J}
is the collection of shortcuts in
X
defined in
6.17
.

Proof.

Let
(F_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be as in
Lemma

8.2

and observe that, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(8.5)

\operatorname{diam}(F_{i+1}-F_{i})(S)=\operatorname{diam}f(S),\qquad S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}.

Indeed, for
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
and
x\in S
there is
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i+1}
with
x\in\partial Q
,
thus
F_{i+1}|_{S}=f|_{S}
, while
\operatorname{diam}F_{i}(S)=0

by
Lemma

8.2
(item (
ii
)).

We now prove the statement.
Fix
i_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{i_{0}}
.
Let
i_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
be the least integer
i\in\mathbb{N}
for which there is
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}

with
S\subseteq Q
.
Since
\operatorname{diam}S=\delta_{i}\sim\theta^{i}
for
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
(see
6.25
and
Eq.

6.1
),
it follows that

(8.6)

\theta^{i_{1}}\lesssim\operatorname{diam}_{X}Q\sim\theta^{i_{0}},

where we have used
Eq.

6.15
of
Lemma

6.14
.
Recall that, by
Lemma

2.28
, there is a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(X,q)\geq 1
such that

(8.7)

(\operatorname{diam}(F_{i+1}-F_{i})(S))^{s}\leq C_{1}L^{s-q}\int_{B(S,C_{1}\delta_{i})}\operatorname{Lip}(F_{i+1}-F_{i};x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{s}(x)

for
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
.
Since
X
is metric doubling and
d(S,S^{\prime})\geq\delta_{i}/2
for
S\neq S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
(see
Lemma

6.21
),
the collection
\{B(S,C_{1}\delta_{i})\colon S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}\}
has uniformly bounded overlap,

\sum_{S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}}\chi_{B(S,C_{1}\delta_{i})}\lesssim_{C_{1},X}1.

Hence, for
i\geq i_{1}
, summing
Eq.

8.7
over
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
with
S\subseteq Q
and recalling
Eq.

8.5
, we have

(8.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}:\\
S\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}(\operatorname{diam}f(S))^{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{X,q}L^{s-q}\int_{B(Q,C_{1}\delta_{i})}\operatorname{Lip}(F_{i+1}-F_{i};x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{s}(x)

\displaystyle\leq L^{s-q}\int_{B(Q,C_{2}\theta^{i_{0}})}\operatorname{Lip}(F_{i+1}-F_{i};x)^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{s}(x),

where we have used
Eq.

8.6
in the last line.
Define

B:=\bigcup\{Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i_{1}}\colon Q^{\prime}\cap B(Q,C_{2}\theta^{i_{0}})\neq\varnothing\}

and observe that, by
Eq.

8.6
and
Eq.

6.15
,

B(Q,C_{2}\theta^{i_{0}})\subseteq B\subseteq B(Q,C_{3}\theta^{i_{0}})
.
In particular,
\mathcal{H}^{s}(B)\sim\mathcal{H}^{s}(Q)
, again by
Eq.

6.15
.
Summing
Eq.

8.8
over
i\geq i_{1}
,
we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{J}:\\
S\subseteq Q\end{subarray}}(\operatorname{diam}f(S))^{s}\lesssim L^{s-q}\sum_{i\geq i_{1}}E_{q}(F_{i+1}-F_{i},B)\lesssim L^{s}K_{q}(Y)^{q}\mathcal{H}^{s}(B),

where the last inequality follows summing both sides of item (
iii
) of
Lemma

8.2

over
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{i_{1}}
with
Q^{\prime}\subseteq B
.
Finally,
\mathcal{H}^{s}(B)\sim\mathcal{H}^{s}(Q)
concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.9
.

The conclusion of
8.3
fails for
s<q
or if the Banach space
Y

does not have a uniformly convex renorming.
Indeed, if it were to hold, the proof of
9.2
would show
that
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
is a
Y
-LDS for any
\eta
,
contradicting the non-differentiability results of
Section

10
.
Alternatively, the maps built in
Section

10
fail
Eq.

8.4
.

9.
Shortcut Laakso spaces

In this short section we combine the results of the previous section to prove the first two points of
1.1
.

Definition 9.1
.

Let
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
be an
s
-ADR Laakso space,
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
, and define
d_{\eta}
as
in
3.5
.
We call
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
a
shortcut Laakso space
.

Recall that
4.1
and
4.3
characterise sequences
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
for
which
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
is PI rectifiable or purely PI unrectifiable.
To prove
1.1
, we will choose
\eta
so that the latter holds.
For instance, any
\eta_{i}\rightarrow 0
with
\sum_{i}\eta_{i}^{s}=\infty
works.

Theorem 9.2
.

Suppose
2\leq q<s
.
Let
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
be a
s
-ADR shortcut Laakso space and
Y
a
q
-uniformly convex Banach space.
Then
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
is a
Y
-LDS with global chart
h\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

By
6.6
and
6.25
,
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
is an
s
-ADR
Y
-LDS with shortcuts compatible with the
global chart
h\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
.
The thesis then follows from
5.18
and
8.3
(and
d_{\eta}\leq d
).
∎

10.
Almost nowhere differentiable Lipschitz maps

In this section, we construct almost nowhere differentiable Lipschitz functions on those shortcut Laakso spaces which are purely PI unrectifiable.
These functions take values in a variety of Banach spaces
Y
, depending on the precise setting.
In particular, we will prove the third and fourth items of
1.1
.

5.6
shows that a function
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
is almost nowhere differentiable if it
separate sufficiently many shortcuts.

3.24
makes ‘sufficiently many’ precise.
In the first lemma, we reduce the construction of a single map
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
to a sequence of building blocks
f_{n}\colon X\to Y
.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
be an
s
-ADR Laakso space,

\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
,

Y
a Banach space,
C\geq 1
, and
(I_{n})_{n\in\mathcal{N}}
a disjoint family of subsets of
\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose there are maps
f_{n}\colon X\to Y
,
n\in\mathcal{N}
, such that:

(i)

for
S\in\bigcup_{i\in I_{n}}\mathcal{J}_{i}
,

C^{-1}\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}S\leq\operatorname{diam}f_{n}(S)\leq C\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}S;

(ii)

for
S\in\mathcal{J}
,
S\notin\bigcup_{i\in I_{n}}\mathcal{J}_{i}
,

\operatorname{diam}f_{n}(S)=0;

(iii)

\sum_{n\in\mathcal{N}}\operatorname{LIP}_{X}(f_{n})<\infty
and
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{N}}\sum_{i\in I_{n}}\eta_{i}^{s}=\infty
.

Then no positive-measure measurable subset of
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
is a
Y
-LDS.

Proof.

By
2.17
, we may assume
Y
has RNP.
Possibly adding a constant to
f_{n}
, we can assume there is
x_{0}\in X
such that
f_{n}(x_{0})=0
for all
n\in\mathcal{N}
.
Hence, from (
iii
),
f:=\sum_{n\in\mathcal{N}}f_{n}
is well-defined and a Lipschitz map from
(X,d)
to
Y
.
Set
I:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathcal{N}}I_{n}
.
We are going to use the following properties of
f
,
implied by items (
i
) and (
ii
)

(10.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{diam}f(S)

\displaystyle\leq C\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}S,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{J},

\displaystyle\operatorname{diam}f(S)

\displaystyle\geq C^{-1}\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}S,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{J}_{i}.

We first show that
f
is Lipschitz also as a map from
(X,d_{\eta})
.
Set
L:=\operatorname{LIP}_{X}(f)<\infty
and let
x,y\in X
.
If
d_{\eta}(x,y)=d(x,y)
, then
\|f(x)-f(y)\|_{Y}\leq Ld_{\eta}(x,y)
.
Suppose
d_{\eta}(x,y)<d(x,y)
and let
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
be such that

(10.3)

4d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq d(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+d(p_{-},y),

where we used
3.11
and
6.25
.
By triangle inequality,
Eq.

10.2
, and
Eq.

10.3
, we have

\displaystyle\|f(x)-f(y)\|_{Y}

\displaystyle\leq Ld(x,p_{-})+\|f(p_{-})-f(p_{+})\|_{Y}+Ld(p_{-},y)

\displaystyle\lesssim Ld(x,p_{-})+\rho_{\eta}(p_{-},p_{+})+Ld(p_{-},y)\lesssim(L+1)d_{\eta}(x,y),

showing that
f\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to Y
is Lipschitz.
Observe that
Eq.

10.2
implies, for all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
,

\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f)\supseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}B_{\eta}(\cup\mathcal{J}_{i},\delta_{i}\eta_{i}/\epsilon).

From (
iii
) and
3.24
, it follows that
\operatorname{Bad}_{\epsilon}(f)

has full
\mathcal{H}^{s}
-measure for all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0

and therefore
\mathcal{H}^{s}(X\setminus\operatorname{Bad}(f))=0
.
The thesis then follows from
5.8

(and
Lemma

2.11
).
∎

We may explicitly construct real valued building blocks as follows.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}

and
X_{i}=X_{i}(M;(N_{n}))
.
There is a
1
-Lipschitz function
f\colon X_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying:

(i)

\operatorname{diam}f(S)=\operatorname{diam}S
for
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
;

(ii)

\operatorname{diam}f(S)=0
for
S\in\bigcup_{j<i}\mathcal{J}_{j}
;

(iii)

f\circ q|_{I\times\{a\}}\colon I\times\{a\}\to\mathbb{R}
is affine linear for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
and
a\in[M]^{i}
;

(iv)

f\circ q|_{\partial I\times[M]^{i}}=0
for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
.

Here
(\mathcal{J}_{j})_{j=1}^{i}
are the families of subsets of
X_{i}
defined in
6.17
.

Proof.

We first construct
\tilde{f}\colon\tilde{X}_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
and
a\in[M]^{i-1}
.
If
\partial I\nsubseteq W_{i}^{h}
, set
\tilde{f}|_{I\times\{a\}\times[M]}:=0
.
Suppose now
\partial I\subseteq W_{i}^{h}
and let
t_{0}
denote the midpoint of
I
.
We define

\tilde{f}(t,a^{\prime},M):=-\tilde{f}(t,a^{\prime},1):=\left(\tfrac{|I|}{2}-|t-t_{0}|\right)^{+},\qquad t\in I,

and
\tilde{f}|_{I\times\{(a^{\prime},a(i))\}}:=0
for
a(i)\neq 1,M
,
where
t^{+}:=\max(0,t)
denotes the positive part of
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since
\tilde{f}|_{\partial I\times\{a\}}=0
for
I\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
and
a\in[M]^{i}
,
\tilde{f}\colon\tilde{X}_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
is well-defined
and induces a unique function
f\colon X_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
f\circ q=\tilde{f}
.
It is easy to verify that
f
has the desired properties.
∎

We also construct building blocks into
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
X_{i}=X_{i}(M;(N_{n}))
.
Let
f\colon X_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
a function such that, for
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
and
a\in[M]^{i}
,

f\circ q|_{J\times\{a\}}\colon J\times\{a\}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ is affine linear.}

Set
L:=\operatorname{LIP}(f)
.
Then, for
A>0
, there is a function
F\colon X_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying:

(i)

\operatorname{diam}F(S)=\operatorname{diam}f(S)
for
S\in\bigcup_{j<i}\mathcal{J}_{j}
;

(ii)

\operatorname{diam}F(S)=A\operatorname{diam}S
for
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
;

(iii)

\operatorname{LIP}(F)^{2}\leq L^{2}+A^{2}
;

(iv)

F\circ q|_{J\times\{a\}}\colon J\times\{a\}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is affine linear for
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
and
a\in[M]^{i}
;

(v)

F\circ q|_{\partial J\times[M]^{i}}=f\circ q|_{\partial J\times[M]^{i}}
for
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
.

Here
(\mathcal{J}_{j})_{j=1}^{i}
are the families of subsets of
X_{i}
defined in
6.17
.

Proof.

We first establish a few properties of
f
.
We can apply
Lemma

2.25
to see that
f
is in in fact Lipschitz, i.e.
L<\infty
.
Next, let
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
with
\partial J\subseteq W^{h}_{i}
and pick
a\in[M]^{i-1}
.
Then, for
a_{1}(i),a_{2}(i)\in[M]
,
f\circ q|_{J\times\{(a,a_{1}(i))\}},f\circ q|_{J\times\{(a,a_{2}(i))\}}
are two affine functions agreeing at the endpoints of the interval
J
.
Hence, there is an affine function
\alpha_{J,a}\colon J\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

(10.6)

f\circ q(t,a,a_{1}(i))=f\circ q(t,a,a_{2}(i))=\alpha_{J,a}(t),\qquad t\in J

and, in particular,
\operatorname{diam}f(S)=0
for
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
.

We now describe the construction of
F
.
For each
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
and
a\in[M]^{i-1}
, we will define a function
F_{J,a}\colon J\times\{a\}\times[M]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

with the property

(10.7)

F_{J,a}|_{\partial J\times\{a\}\times[M]}=f\circ q|_{\partial J\times\{a\}\times[M]}.

The function in the thesis will then be the unique
F\colon X_{i}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying

F\circ q|_{J\times\{a\}\times[M]}=F_{J,a}
for all
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
and
a\in[M]^{i-1}
;
its existence easily follows from
Eq.

10.7
.
Also, (
10.7
) implies (
v
),
which in turn gives (
i
), because
for
S\in\mathcal{J}_{j}
,
j<i
, there is
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
with
S\subseteq q(\partial J\times[M]^{i})
.
By
Lemma

2.25
, we know that (
iii
) holds as soon as

(10.8)

\operatorname{LIP}(F_{J,a})^{2}\leq L^{2}+A^{2}

for all
J,a
.
Hence, during the construction of
\{F_{J,a}\}
, we need only to ensure the validity of
Eq.

10.7
,
Eq.

10.8
and items
(
ii
), (
iv
).

We are ready to proceed with the construction.
Let
g\colon X_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
be the
1
-Lipschitz function given by
Lemma

10.4
.
Let
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
.
If
\partial J\nsubseteq W_{i}^{h}
, then
J\cap\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}=\varnothing
and so
q(J\times[M]^{i})\cap S=\varnothing
for
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
.
We set
F_{J,a}:=f\circ q|_{J\times\{a\}\times[M]}
for each
a\in[M]^{i-1}
.
With this choice,
Eq.

10.7
,
Eq.

10.8
and
items (
ii
), (
iv
) are clearly satisfied.

Suppose now
\partial J\subseteq W^{h}_{i}
and let
a\in[M]^{i-1}
.
Let
\alpha_{J,a}\colon J\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be as in
Eq.

10.6
and
choose
v_{J,a}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
such that

(10.9)

\langle v_{J,a},\alpha_{J,a}(t_{1})-\alpha_{J,a}(t_{2})\rangle=0,\qquad t_{1},t_{2}\in J.

(Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the standard scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.)
We set
F_{J,a}:=(f\circ q+Ag\circ qv_{J,a})|_{J\times\{a\}\times[M]}
.
By
Lemma

10.4
we know that
g
satisfies the condition of item (
iv
)
and
g\circ q|_{\partial J\times[M]^{i}}=0
.
It follows that
F_{J,a}
satisfies (
iv
) and
Eq.

10.7
.
By
Lemma

10.4
and
Eq.

10.6
it is clear that (
ii
) is also satisfied.
It remains to show
Eq.

10.8
.
Let
t_{1},t_{2}\in J
,
a_{1}(i),a_{2}(i)\in[M]
, and set
x_{k}:=(t_{k},a,a_{k}(i))
,
1\leq k\leq 2
.
Then
Eq.

10.6
and
Eq.

10.9
imply

\displaystyle\|F_{J,a}(t_{1},a_{1}(i))-F_{J,a}(t_{2},a_{2}(i))\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|f\circ q(x_{1})-f\circ q(x_{2})\|^{2}+A^{2}\|g\circ q(x_{1})-g\circ q(x_{2})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(L^{2}+A^{2})d(q(x_{1}),q(x_{2}))^{2}.

As discussed, this concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 10.10
.

There is a constant
C\geq 1
such that the following holds.
For any Laakso space
X
,
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
, and
non-empty
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, there is a function
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying:

(i)

C^{-1}\leq\operatorname{diam}f(S)/\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}S\leq C
for
S\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{J}_{i}
;

(ii)

\operatorname{diam}f(S)=0
for
S\in\mathcal{J}
,
S\notin\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{J}_{i}
;

(iii)

\operatorname{LIP}_{X}(f)^{2}\leq\sum_{i\in I}\eta_{i}^{2}
.

Proof.

We first establish the following claim.
For any (possibly finite) strictly increasing sequence
(i_{k})_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{N}

there are functions
f_{k}\colon X_{i_{k}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying:

(1)

\operatorname{diam}f_{k}(S)=\eta_{i_{l}}\operatorname{diam}S
for
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i_{l}}
,
l\leq k
;

(2)

\operatorname{diam}f_{k}(S)=0
for
S\in\bigcup_{j\leq i_{k}}\mathcal{J}_{j}
,
S\notin\bigcup_{l\leq k}\mathcal{J}_{i_{l}}
;

(3)

\operatorname{LIP}(f_{k})^{2}\leq\sum_{l\leq k}\eta_{i_{l}}^{2}
;

(4)

f_{k}\circ q|_{J\times\{a\}}
is affine for
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{k}+1}
and
a\in[M]^{i_{k}}
;

(5)

f_{k}\circ q|_{\partial J\times[M]^{i_{k}}}=f_{k-1}\circ\pi^{i_{k}}_{i_{k-1}}\circ q|_{\partial J\times[M]^{i_{k}}}
for
J\in\mathcal{I}_{i_{k}}
.

If
g\colon X_{i_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}
is the function given by
Lemma

10.4
,
then
f_{1}:=(\eta_{i_{1}}g,0)\colon X_{i_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
has all the desired properties.
Hence, if
(i_{k})_{k}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
is a (possibly finite) strictly increasing sequence of
N
terms,
N\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
we can at least find
f_{1}\colon X_{i_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Suppose now
1<n\leq N
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and that we can find
f_{1},\dots,f_{n-1}
as in the claim.
We show that we can then construct
f_{n}\colon X_{i_{n}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying (1-5).
Since
i_{n-1}<i_{n}
, it is not difficult to see that
f_{n-1}\circ\pi^{i_{n}}_{i_{n-1}}\colon X_{i_{n}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfies the assumption
of
Lemma

10.5
with
i\equiv i_{n}
.
Taking
A\equiv\eta_{i_{n}}
, the lemma gives a function
f_{n}\colon X_{i_{n}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying (1-5), hence proving the claim.

We can now prove the lemma.
If
I
is finite,
I=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{n}\}
, we can take
f:=f_{n}\circ\pi^{\infty}_{i_{n}}
, where
f_{n}
is as in the claim.
Suppose now
I
is infinite and let
i_{k}\uparrow\infty
be such that
I=\{i_{k}\colon k\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
We can assume

(10.11)

L:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\eta_{i_{k}}^{2}<\infty,

for otherwise the statement is trivial.
Let
\tilde{f}_{k}\colon X_{i_{k}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the function provided by the claim and set
f_{k}:=\tilde{f}_{k}\circ\pi^{\infty}_{i_{k}}\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
By item (5),
(f_{k})
converges pointwise on the set

E:=q\left(\left\{\frac{m}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}}\colon n\in\mathbb{N},m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},0\leq m\leq N_{1}\cdots N_{n}\right\}\times[M]^{\mathbb{N}}\right).

By item (3) and
Eq.

10.11
,
(f_{k})
is equicontinuous.
Since
E
is dense in
X
, it follows that
f_{k}
converges pointwise on
X
to an
L
-Lipschitz function
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
By pointwise convergence and (1-2) (or by (1-2) and (5)), we conclude that
f
satisfies also (
i
),(
ii
).
∎

We are now in a position to construct non-differentiable Banach space valued functions on shortcut Laakso spaces.

For
q\in[1,\infty]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote
\ell_{q}^{n}=(\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{q}})
.
We say that a Banach space
Y

contains
\ell_{q}^{n}
uniformly

if there are
C\geq 1
and, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, linear operators
T_{n}\colon\ell_{q}^{n}\to Y

satisfying

(10.12)

C^{-1}\|x\|_{\ell_{q}^{n}}\leq\|T_{n}x\|_{Y}\leq C\|x\|_{\ell_{q}^{n}}

for all
x\in\ell_{q}^{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By a theorem of Krivine
[
Kri75
]

(see
[
MS86
, 10.5]
for the precise statement we use),
we may equivalently require
Eq.

10.12
for all
C>1
.

Proposition 10.13
.

Suppose
2\leq s<q<\infty
.
Let
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
be an
s
-ADR shortcut Laakso space, assume it is purely PI unrectifiable,
and let
Y
be a Banach space containing
\ell_{q}^{n}
uniformly (e.g.
Y=\ell_{q}
).
Then, no positive-measure measurable subset of
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
is a
Y
-LDS.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.22
, we find
countably many finite non-empty disjoint sets
I_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that

(10.14)

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Big(\sum_{i\in I_{n}}\eta_{i}^{q}\Big)^{1/q}<\infty,\qquad\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{i\in I_{n}}\eta_{i}^{s}=\infty.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote with
N_{n}
the cardinality of
I_{n}
.
We will first construct a sequence of functions
\tilde{f}_{n}\colon X\to\ell_{q}^{N_{n}}
satisfying items
(
i
), (
ii
), (
iii
)
of
Lemma

10.1
.
It will then be easy to produce a sequence
f_{n}\colon X\to Y
with the same property.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

i\in I_{n}
, let
g_{i}\colon X_{i}\to\mathbb{R}
be given by
Lemma

10.4
and set

\tilde{f}_{n}:=(\eta_{i}g_{i}\circ\pi_{i}^{\infty})_{i\in I_{n}}\colon X\to(\mathbb{R}^{I_{n}},\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{q}})\equiv\ell_{q}^{N_{n}}
.
Items (
ii
) and (
iii
) follow easily from

Lemma

10.4
, the definition of
\tilde{f}_{n}
, and
Eq.

10.14
.
Recall that by
Lemma

3.23
and
6.25
,
it holds

\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}S\sim\eta_{i}\delta_{i}\sim\eta_{i}\operatorname{diam}S,\qquad S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}.

Hence, also item (
i
) follows from
Lemma

10.4
and the definition of
\tilde{f}_{n}
.

Since
N_{n}
is finite and
Y
contains
\ell_{q}^{n}
uniformly, there are a constant
C\geq 1

and linear operators
T_{n}\colon\ell_{q}^{N_{n}}\to Y
satisfying

Eq.

10.12
.
It is then clear that the maps
f_{n}:=T_{n}\circ\tilde{f}_{n}\colon X\to Y
satisfy the assumptions of
Lemma

10.1
,
from which the thesis follows.
∎

10.13
also holds if
1<s<2
.
However, for this range we can establish a much stronger conclusion,
namely that no positive-measure measurable subset of
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
is an LDS.

Proposition 10.15
.

Suppose
1<s<2
.
Let
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
be an
s
-ADR shortcut Laakso space and assume it is purely PI unrectifiable.
Then, no positive-measure measurable subset of
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
is an LDS.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.22
,
\eta=(\eta_{i})
has a subsequence
(\eta_{i_{j}})_{j}

satisfying

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\eta_{i_{j}}^{2}<\infty,\qquad\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\eta_{i_{j}}^{s}=\infty.

Let
I:=\{i_{j}\colon j\in\mathbb{N}\}
and
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be given by
Lemma

10.10
.
The single map
f
and set
I
satisfy the assumptions of
Lemma

10.1
,
concluding the proof.
∎

The following is our final non-differentiability result.

Proposition 10.16
.

Let
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
be an
s
-ADR shortcut Laakso space, suppose it is purely PI unrectifiable,
and let
Y
be a non-superreflexive Banach space.
Then, no positive-measure measurable subset of
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
is a
Y
-LDS.

The proof relies on the following theorem, whose proof is postponed to the next section.

Theorem 10.17
.

Let
Y
be a Banach space and
X
a Laakso space.
Then
Y
is non-superreflexive if and only if there is
C\geq 1
such that
for any finite non-empty set
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
there is a
1
-Lipschitz function
f\colon X\to Y
satisfying:

(i)

\operatorname{diam}f(S)\geq C^{-1}\operatorname{diam}S
for
S\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{J}_{i}
;

(ii)

\operatorname{diam}f(S)=0
for
S\in\mathcal{J}
,
S\notin\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{J}_{i}
.

Moreover, if
Y
is non-superreflexive, the above holds for any
C>8
.

Proof of
10.16
.

By
Lemma

2.22
, there is a subsequence
(\eta_{i_{j}})_{j}
such that
\eta_{i_{j}}\rightarrow 0
and
\sum_{j}\eta_{i_{j}}^{s}=\infty
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, set

I_{n}:=\{i_{j}\colon 2^{-n}<\eta_{i_{j}}\leq 2^{1-n}\}

and note that they are all finite sets.
Let
\tilde{f}_{n}
be the map given by
10.17
with
I\equiv I_{n}

and
define
f_{n}:=2^{-n}\tilde{f}_{n}
(or
f_{n}:=0
if
I_{n}=\varnothing
).
To conclude, it is enough to verify that
f_{n}
and
I_{n}
satisfy the assumptions of
Lemma

10.1
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
i\in I_{n}
and
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
, we have

\operatorname{diam}f_{n}(S)\sim\eta_{i}\operatorname{diam}S\sim\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}S,

while if
i\notin I_{n}
,
S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
,
Lemma

10.1
gives
\operatorname{diam}f_{n}(S)=0
.
Lastly,
\sum_{n}\operatorname{LIP}_{X}(f_{n})\leq 1
and

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{i\in I_{n}}\eta_{i}^{s}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\eta_{i_{j}}^{s}=\infty

conclude the proof.
∎

11.
A characterisation of non-superreflexive spaces

The goal of this section is to prove
10.17
.
We exploit well-known characterisations of non-superreflexivity in terms of biLipschitz embeddability of families of graphs,
first introduced by Bourgain
[
Bou86
]
; see also
[
Bau07
]
.
More specifically, we (indirectly) use a technique introduced in
[
OR17
]

and later applied in
[
Swi18
]
to a larger family of graphs.

The above works consider ‘Laakso graphs’.
However, in spite of the shared name, none of the above works deal with the Laakso spaces defined in
Section

6
nor their finite approximations
G_{n}
in
Section

6.1
.
Indeed,
[
OR17
,
Swi18
]
consider
the family of graphs introduced in
[
LP01
, Section 2]
which is inspired by
[
Laa02
]
and
are often called ‘Laakso graphs’; their Gromov-Hausdorff limit is also sometimes called ‘Laakso space’.
However, the spaces we are considering, those of
[
Laa00
]
, are not directly related to those of
[
Laa02
]
.
For instance, all of the latter graphs
are planar, while
G_{n}
is non-planar for
n\geq 2
and
M\geq 3
(since it contains
K_{3,3}
).
Nonetheless, the techniques of
[
OR17
]
can be used to construct the map required in
10.17
.
Instead of doing so directly, we shall reduce the construction of our map to the embedding of a graph simpler than
G_{n}
,
for which
[
Swi18
]
can be applied.

We refer the reader to
[
Swi18
, Definitions 2.1 and 2.4]
for the definition of
M
-branching bundle graph
and to
[
Die25
]
for graph theory background.
We stress that, compared to
[
Swi18
]
, we adopt a slightly different vertex set, with the only difference being a different scaling of the ‘height’ coordinate.
We also scale the distance accordingly.

11.1.
Diamond graphs

We define a collection of graphs which we call
diamond graphs
.
Let
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
M\geq 2
,
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
and set

(11.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{W}_{1}^{h}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{W}_{1}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{n}):=W_{1}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{n})\cup\{0,1\},

\displaystyle\widehat{W}_{j}^{h}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{W}_{j}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{n}):=W_{j}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{n}),

\displaystyle\widehat{W}_{\leq l}^{h}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{W}_{\leq l}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{n}):=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}\widehat{W}_{i}^{h}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{m}{N_{1}\cdots N_{l}}\colon m\in\mathbb{N}_{0},0\leq m\leq N_{1}\dots N_{l}\right\}

for
1<j\leq n
and
1\leq l\leq n
.
For
1\leq j\leq n
and
t\in\widehat{W}_{j}^{h}
, set
w_{t}:=j-1
.
We then denote with

(11.2)

\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})

the
M
-branching bundle graph associated with
(w_{t})
; see
[
Swi18
, Definition 2.9]
.

Our first main goal is to show that
[
Swi18
]
implies the existence of biLipschitz embeddings of our diamond graphs
with uniformly bounded distortion.
Swift proves that suitable graphs, including our diamond graphs
\widehat{G}
, admit biLipschitz embeddings in non-superreflexive Banach space.
The distortion of such embeddings depends
on a geometric parameter
p_{\widehat{G}}
of the graph, which we now define.

Let
\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
and define for
t\in\widehat{W}^{h}_{\leq n}
,
t=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tfrac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
, and
1\leq l\leq n

(11.3)

\displaystyle x(t,l)

\displaystyle:=t-d([0,t]\cap\widehat{W}^{h}_{\leq l},t)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq l}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}},

\displaystyle y(t,l)

\displaystyle:=t+d([t,1]\cap\widehat{W}^{h}_{\leq l},t)=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{l}}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq l}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}},

and
x(t,0):=0
,
y(t,0):=1
.
We also set
x(t,l):=y(t,l):=t
for
l>n
6
6

6

The definitions of
x(t,l),y(t,l)
we adopt differs slightly from the one of
[
Swi18
]
, but
it is not difficult to see that they are equivalent.

.
Observe that equality between the leftmost and rightmost sides of
Eq.

11.3
holds even for
l=0
, provided we interpret empty sums as
0
and empty products as
1
.
Let
p_{\widehat{G}}
be the least
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
0\leq j<n
and
t\in\widehat{W}_{\leq n}^{h}

•

t\geq(x(t,j)+y(t,j))/2
implies
x(t,j+p)\geq(x(t,j)+y(t,j))/2
;

•

t\leq(x(t,j)+y(t,j))/2
implies
y(t,j+p)\leq(x(t,j)+y(t,j))/2
.

We shall verify that
p_{\widehat{G}}=1
.

Remark 11.4
.

For
t\in\widehat{W}_{j}^{h}
it holds
x(t,l)=y(t,l)=t
,
j\leq l\leq n
.
We may then restrict our attention to
1\leq l+1<j\leq n
.
Also, since
N_{l+1}
is even,
(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2\in\widehat{W}^{h}_{l+1}
for
0\leq l<n

and in particular
t\neq(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
whenever
t\notin\widehat{W}_{l+1}^{h}
.

Lemma 11.5
.

Let
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
M\geq 2
,
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
,
and recall the definitions
Eqs.

11.1
and
11.3
.
Let
1\leq l+1<j\leq n
and
t\in\widehat{W}_{j}^{h}
,
t=\sum_{i=1}^{j}\tfrac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
.
Then

t>(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
if and only if
t_{l+1}\geq N_{l+1}/2
.

Proof.

Under the present assumptions
t\neq(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
; see
11.4
.
To treat the cases
l=0
and
l>0
in a unified way, we adopt the conventions on empty sums and product mentioned after
Eq.

11.3
.
Observe that
t\geq(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
if and only if

\sum_{l<i\leq j}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\geq\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{l}}.

Now, if
t_{l+1}<N_{l+1}/2
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{l<i\leq j}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t_{l+1}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{l+1}}+\sum_{i=l+2}^{j}\frac{N_{i}-1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}<\frac{t_{l+1}+1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{l+1}}\leq\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{l}},

proving
t<(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
.
If instead
t_{l+1}\geq N_{l+1}/2
, then

\sum_{l<i\leq j}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\geq\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{l}},

proving
t>(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
.
∎

Lemma 11.6
.

Let
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
M\geq 2
,
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
,
consider
\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
,
and recall the definitions
Eqs.

11.1
and
11.3
.
Let
0\leq l<n
and
t\in\widehat{W}_{\leq n}^{h}
. Then

•

t\geq(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
implies
x(t,l+1)\geq(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
;

•

t\leq(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
implies
y(t,l+1)\leq(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
.

Equivalently,
p_{\widehat{G}}=1
.

Proof.

Let
1\leq j\leq n
be such that
t\in\widehat{W}_{j}^{h}
and write
t=\sum_{i=1}^{j}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
.
Assume w.l.o.g.
j>l+1
and hence
t\neq(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
; see
11.4
.
The thesis will follow from
Eq.

11.3
,
Lemma

11.5
and some simple computations.
We adopt the conventions on empty sums and product mentioned after
Eq.

11.3
.
If
t>(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
, then
t_{l+1}\geq N_{l+1}/2
and therefore

x(t,l+1)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq l+1}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\geq\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{l}}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq l}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{1}}=(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2.

If instead
t<(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2
, then
t_{l+1}<N_{l+1}/2
and therefore

y(t,l+1)=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{l+1}}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq l+1}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\leq\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{l}}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq l}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}=(x(t,l)+y(t,l))/2.

Recalling the definition of
p_{\widehat{G}}
(see the paragraph after
Eq.

11.3
), we immediately have
p_{\widehat{G}}=1
.
∎

Theorem 11.7

(
[
Swi18
, Theorem 5.4]
)
.

Let
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
M\geq 2
,
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
, and let
\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
.
Then, for any non-superreflexive Banach space
Y
and
\epsilon>0
, there is a map
f\colon\widehat{G}\to Y
such that

(11.8)

(8+\epsilon)^{-1}d_{\widehat{G}}(u,v)\leq\|f(u)-f(v)\|_{Y}\leq d_{\widehat{G}}(u,v),

for
u,v
in
\widehat{G}
.

Proof.

The thesis follows from
[
Swi18
, Theorem 5.4]
and
[
BS75
]
, as we now describe.
We refer to
[
Swi18
, Definition 5.1]
or
[
OR17
, Definition 2.1]
for the definition of equal-sign-additive (ESA) basis
and
[
Pis16
, Chapter 11]
for finite representability.

Since
Y
is non-superreflexive, there is a non-reflexive Banach space
Z_{1}
which is finitely representable in
Y
.
By
[
BS75
]
(see the paragraph after
[
OR17
, Theorem 2.3]
for details),
there is a Banach space
Z
with an equal-sign-additive (ESA) basis which is finitely representable in
Z_{1}

and hence in
Y
.
Thus,
[
Swi18
, Theorem 5.4]
and
Lemma

11.6
ensure the existence of a map
g\colon\widehat{G}\to Z
satisfying

\frac{1}{8}d_{\widehat{G}}(u,v)\leq\|g(u)-g(v)\|_{Z}\leq d_{\widehat{G}}(u,v),

for
u,v
in
\widehat{G}
.
Since
\widehat{G}
is finite, the thesis follows by definition of finite representability.
∎

To prove
10.17
, we need to define suitable ‘projections’ between diamond graphs
and establish some basic properties.

Let
n,M\geq 2
,
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
and let
(\widehat{V},\widehat{E})=\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
denote the corresponding diamond graph.
Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
I=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\}
be a subset of
[n-1]
.
For
t\in\widehat{W}^{h}_{\leq n}
, set
w_{I,t}:=\#\{j\in[k]\colon i_{j}\leq w_{t}\}
,

\widehat{V}_{I}:=\bigcup\left\{{\{t\}\times[M]^{w_{I,t}}\colon t\in\widehat{W}_{\leq n}^{h}}\right\}

and

(11.9)

\pi_{I}\colon\widehat{V}\to\widehat{V}_{I},\qquad\pi_{I}(t,a):=(t,a|_{I}).

Set
i_{0}:=0
,
i_{k+1}:=n
, and for
1\leq j\leq k+1

(11.10)

\widehat{W}_{I,j}^{h}:=\bigcup_{l=i_{j-1}+1}^{i_{j}}\widehat{W}_{l}^{h},\qquad N_{I,j}:=\prod_{i=i_{j-1}+1}^{i_{j}}N_{i}.

Observe that
N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,k+1}\in 2\mathbb{N}
and

\widehat{W}_{I,j}^{h}=\widehat{W}_{j}^{h}(N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,k+1}).

It is then clear that
w_{I,t}=j-1
for
t\in\widehat{W}_{I,j}^{h}
,
1\leq j\leq k+1
.
Hence,
\widehat{V}_{I}
coincides with the vertex set of
\widehat{G}(M;N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,k+1})
.
Let
\widehat{E}_{I}
denote the smallest edge set for which
\pi_{I}\colon\widehat{G}\to(\widehat{V}_{I},\widehat{E}_{I})
is a graph homomorphism
and define

(11.11)

\widehat{G}_{I}:=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})_{I}:=(\widehat{V}_{I},\widehat{E}_{I}).

Lemma 11.12
.

Let
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
n,M\geq 2
,
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
, and let
\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
.
Let
I=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\}\subseteq[n-1]
be a non-empty set and define

N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,k+1}\in 2\mathbb{N}
and
\widehat{G}_{I}
as in
Eq.

11.10
and
Eq.

11.11
respectively.
Then

\widehat{G}_{I}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})_{I}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,k+1}).

Proof.

We use the notation
\preceq
from
[
Swi18
, Section 2]
.
Let
u=(t,a),v=(s,b)\in\widehat{V}_{I}
.
We may assume
t\in\widehat{W}_{l(t)}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
,
s\in\widehat{W}_{l(s)}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
with

1\leq l(t)\leq l(s)\leq n
.
Suppose
u,v
are adjacent in
\widehat{G}(M;N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,k+1})
, i.e.
|t-s|=\tfrac{1}{N_{I,1}\cdots N_{I,k+1}}=\tfrac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}}
and
a\preceq b
or
b\preceq a
.
Since
l(t)\leq l(s)
, we have
w_{I,l(t)}\leq w_{I,l(s)}
, and therefore it must be
a\preceq b
.
The conditions
l(t)\leq l(s)
and
a\preceq b
guarantee the existence of
a_{0},b_{0}
with
a_{0}\preceq b_{0}
or
b_{0}\preceq a_{0}

and
\pi_{I}(t,a)=(t,a_{0})
,
\pi_{I}(s,b)=(s,b_{0})
,
proving that
u,v
are adjacent also in
\widehat{G}_{I}
.
Suppose now
u,v
are adjacent in
\widehat{G}_{I}
, i.e. there are
(t,a_{0}),(s,b_{0})
adjacent in
\widehat{G}
such that
u=\pi_{I}(t,a_{0})
and
v=\pi_{I}(s,b_{0})
.
Then
|t-s|=\tfrac{1}{N_{I,1}\cdots N_{I,k+1}}=\tfrac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}}
,
a_{0}\preceq b_{0}
,
hence
a\preceq b
and therefore
u,v
are adjacent in
\widehat{G}(M;N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,k+1})
.
∎

Recall the notation introduced in
Eq.

6.18
.

Lemma 11.13
.

Let
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
n,M\geq 2
, and
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
.
Let
I=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\}\subseteq[n-1]
be a non-empty set and define
N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,k+1}
as in
Eq.

11.10
.
Then, for
1\leq j\leq k
, we have

\mathcal{J}_{i_{j}}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{i_{j}})\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{j}^{h}(N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,j}).

Proof.

Let
t\in\mathcal{J}_{i_{j}}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{i_{j}})
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
0\leq t_{i}<N_{i}
for
1\leq i<i_{j}
and
0<t_{i_{j}}<N_{i_{j}}-1
such that

t=\sum_{i=1}^{i_{j}}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}+\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i_{j}}}.

There are unique
\tau_{l}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
0\leq\tau_{l}<N_{I,l}
, satisfying

\frac{\tau_{l}}{N_{I,l}}=\sum_{i=i_{l-1}+1}^{i_{l}}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}

for
1\leq l\leq j
.
Since
0<t_{i_{j}}<N_{i_{j}}-1
, we have
\tau_{j}>0
and

\tau_{j}=\left[\sum_{i=i_{j-1}+1}^{i_{j}}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\right](N_{i_{j-1}+1}\cdots N_{i_{j}})<N_{i_{j-1}+1}\cdots N_{i_{j}}-1,

i.e.
\tau_{j}<N_{I,j}-1
.
Hence,
t
can be written as

t=\sum_{l=1}^{j}\frac{\tau_{l}}{N_{I,1}\cdots N_{I,l}}+\frac{1}{2N_{I,1}\cdots N_{I,j}}

with
0\leq\tau_{l}<N_{I,l}
and
0<\tau_{j}<N_{I,j}-1
, proving
t\in\mathcal{J}^{h}_{j}(N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,j})
.
∎

Let
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
n,M\geq 2
,
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
, and
\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
.
We then set, for
1\leq i\leq n-1
,

(11.14)

\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{i}:=\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{i}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{i}):=\big\{\{t\}\times\{a\}\times[M]\colon t\in\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{i})\text{ and }a\in[M]^{i-1}\big\}.

Lemma 11.15
.

Let
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
n,M\geq 2
,
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
, and
\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
.
For
1\leq i\leq n-1
,
u\neq v\in S\in\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
, we have

d_{\widehat{G}}(u,v)=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}.

Proof.

Write
u=(t,a,u(i))
,
v=(t,a,v(i))
, for some
a\in[M]^{i-1}
and
t\in\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}\subseteq\widehat{W}_{i+1}^{h}
.
Recall that in the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}
we require
t\pm\tfrac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}\in W_{i}^{h}

and so, for
1\leq j\leq i
,

d_{\mathbb{R}}(t,\widehat{W}_{j}^{h})\geq\frac{1}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}=d_{\mathbb{R}}(t,\widehat{W}_{i}^{h})=d_{\mathbb{R}}(t,W_{i}^{h}).

Since
u,v
differ exactly in the
i
-th digit,
from
[
Swi18
, Proposition 2.9]
we then have

d_{\widehat{G}}(u,v)=\frac{2}{2N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}.

∎

Lemma 11.16
.

Let
n,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
n,M\geq 2
,
N_{1},\dots,N_{n}\in 2\mathbb{N}
, and
\widehat{G}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n})
.
Let
I\subseteq[n-1]
be a non-empty set and let
\widehat{G}_{I}
and
\pi_{I}\colon\widehat{G}\to\widehat{G}_{I}
be as in

Eq.

11.11
and
Eq.

11.9
respectively.
Then the map
\pi_{I}
is
1
-Lipschitz and
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
,
u,v\in S\in\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
, it holds

d_{\widehat{G}_{I}}(\pi_{I}(u),\pi_{I}(v))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}d_{\widehat{G}}(u,v),&i\in I\\
0,&i\notin I\end{array}\right..

Proof.

Since
\pi_{I}
is a graph homomorphism, it follows that it is
1
-Lipschitz.
Let
I=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\}
and
N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,k+1}
be as in
Eq.

11.10
.
We can assume
u\neq v
and write

u=(t,a,u(i))
,
v=(t,a,v(i))
, for some
a\in[M]^{i-1}
and
t\in\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}(N_{1},\dots,N_{i})
.
Suppose
i\in I
and let
1\leq j\leq k
be such that
i=i_{j}
.
Then
\pi_{I}(S)=\{(t,a|_{I})\}\times[M]
with
a|_{I}\in[M]^{j-1}
and
t\in\mathcal{J}_{j}^{h}(N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,j})

(by
Lemma

11.13
).
Hence,
\pi_{I}(u)\neq\pi_{I}(v)\in\pi_{I}(S)\in\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{j}(M;N_{I,1},\dots,N_{I,j})
and so

Lemma

11.15
and
Eq.

11.10
yield

d_{\widehat{G}_{I}}(\pi_{I}(u),\pi_{I}(v))=\frac{1}{N_{I,1}\cdots N_{I,j}}=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i_{j}}}=d_{\widehat{G}}(u,v).

If
i\notin I
,
from
1\leq i\leq n-1
we find
1\leq j\leq k+1
such that
i_{j-1}<i<i_{j}
and therefore

\pi_{I}(u)=(t,a|_{I})=\pi_{I}(v)
.
∎

11.2.
Projection of Laakso graphs onto diamond graphs

We use the following notation for the rest of the section.
Fix a Laakso space
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
and, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

G_{n}
be the graph having
X_{n}=X_{n}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n+1})
as metric graph (see
Section

6.1
),
and set
\widehat{G}_{n}=\widehat{G}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{n+1})
(
Eq.

11.2
).
We let
\widehat{\pi}_{n}
denote the obvious projection from the vertex set of
G_{n}
onto the one of
\widehat{G}_{n}
.
For a non-empty
I\subseteq[n]
, set
\widehat{G}_{n,I}:=(\widehat{G}_{n})_{I}
(
Eq.

11.11
)
and
\widehat{\pi}_{n,I}:=\pi_{I}\circ\widehat{\pi}_{n}
, where
\pi_{I}
is as in
Eq.

11.9
.

Lemma 11.17
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
I\subseteq[n]
be a non-empty set.
Let
G_{n}
,
\widehat{G}_{n,I}
, and
\widehat{\pi}_{n,I}\colon G_{n}\to\widehat{G}_{n,I}
be as above.
Then the map
\widehat{\pi}_{n,I}
is
1
-Lipschitz and for
1\leq i\leq n
and
u,v\in S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
, we have

d_{\widehat{G}_{n,I}}(\widehat{\pi}_{n,I}(u),\widehat{\pi}_{n,I}(v))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}d_{G_{n}}(u,v),&i\in I\\
0,&i\notin I\end{array}\right..

Proof.

It is not difficult to see that
\widehat{\pi}_{n}\colon G_{n}\to\widehat{G}_{n}
is a graph homomorphism and therefore
1
-Lipschitz.
Thus,
Lemma

11.16
shows that
\widehat{\pi}_{n,I}
is
1
-Lipschitz.
Let
S
be as in the statement and assume w.l.o.g.
u\neq v\in S
.
From the definitions of
G_{n}
,
\widehat{G}_{n}
, and
\widehat{\pi}_{n}\colon G_{n}\to\widehat{G}_{n}
, we have

\widehat{\pi}_{n}(u)\neq\widehat{\pi}_{n}(v)\in\widehat{\pi}_{n}(S)\in\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{i}(M;N_{1},\dots,N_{i})
(
Eq.

11.14
).
Hence,
Lemma

11.15
and (the argument of)
Lemma

6.22
give

d_{\widehat{G}_{n}}(\widehat{\pi}_{n}(u),\widehat{\pi}_{n}(v))=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}=d_{G_{n}}(u,v).

Finally,
Lemma

11.16
concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of
10.17
.

Suppose first
Y
is non-superreflexive,
set
n:=\max(I)
, and let
G_{n},\widehat{G}_{n,I}
, and
\widehat{\pi}_{n,I}\colon G_{n}\to\widehat{G}_{n,I}
be as above.
By
Lemma

11.12
and
11.7
, we deduce the
existence of a map
\widehat{f}\colon\widehat{G}_{n,I}\to Y
for which
Eq.

11.8
holds.
Set
\tilde{f}:=\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{\pi}_{n,I}\colon G_{n}\to Y
and observe that, by
Lemma

11.17
, it is
1
-Lipschitz
and satisfies both items of
10.17
(on the graph) for
1\leq i\leq n
.
With small abuse of notation, let
\tilde{f}\colon X_{n}\to Y
denote the extension of
\tilde{f}|_{G_{n}}
to
X_{n}
given by
Lemma

6.9
,
and finally set
f:=\tilde{f}\circ\pi_{n}^{\infty}\colon X\to Y
.
Since
\operatorname{diam}\pi_{n}^{\infty}(S)=0
for
S\in\bigcup_{i>n}\mathcal{J}_{i}
, the map
f
is as claimed.

For the converse direction, we use the differentiability theory of Cheeger-Kleiner
[
CK09
]

and a characterisation of superreflexivity via ultrapowers.
We refer the reader to
[
Hei80
]
for background on ultrafilters, ultrapowers,
and their applications to Banach space theory.

Suppose
Y
is a Banach space,
C\geq 1
, and that, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
there is a
1
-Lipschitz map
f_{n}\colon X\to Y
satisfying (
i
) with
I=[n]
.
We may assume w.l.o.g.
\sup_{n}\|f_{n}\|_{\infty}<\infty
.
Let
\mathcal{U}
be a non-principal ultrafilter on
\mathbb{N}
, let
Y^{\mathcal{U}}
denote the corresponding ultrapower of
Y
,
and define
f\colon X\to Y^{\mathcal{U}}

as
f(x):=[(f_{n}(x))]
,
where, for bounded
(y_{n})\subseteq Y
,
[(y_{n})]\in Y^{\mathcal{U}}
denotes its equivalence class.
Since
\mathcal{U}
is non-principal and
\|[(y_{n})]\|_{Y^{\mathcal{U}}}=\lim_{\mathcal{U}}\|y_{n}\|_{Y}
for
(y_{n})\subseteq Y
bounded,
it is not difficult to verify that
f
is
1
-Lipschitz and
\operatorname{diam}f(S)\geq C^{-1}\operatorname{diam}S

for
S\in\mathcal{J}
.
Then, from
Lemma

10.1
with the single map
f
and
\eta=(1,1,\dots)
,
we deduce that no positive-measure measurable subset of
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
is a
Y^{\mathcal{U}}
-LDS,
where
s
denotes the Hausdorff dimension of
X
.
Since
(X,d,\mathcal{H}^{s})
is a PI space, by
[
CK09
, Theorem 1.5]

we see that
Y^{\mathcal{U}}
does not have RNP and therefore it is not reflexive
[
BL00
, Corollary 5.12(ii)]
.
Finally, by
[
Hei80
, Proposition 6.4]
,
we conclude that
Y
is non-superreflexive.
∎

12.
Lipschitz dimension and biLipschitz embeddability

The notion of Lipschitz dimension was introduced in
[
CK13
]
, where Cheeger and Kleiner prove
a structure theorem for metric spaces of Lipschitz dimension at most
1
(and, more generally, of Lipschitz light maps)
and their biLipschitz embeddability into
L^{1}
spaces.
It was further studied in
[
Dav21
,
Dav24
]
.

In this section we prove that any shortcut Laakso space
(X,d_{\eta})

has topological, Nagata, and Lipschitz dimension
1
(
12.26
).
As a corollary, all such spaces admit a biLipschitz embedding in
L^{1}([0,1])
,
and a short argument yields the non-embeddability claimed in
1.1
.

We begin focusing on Lipschitz dimension.

Definition 12.1
.

Given
r\in(0,\infty)
, a finite sequence
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
of points in
X
is an
r
-chain
or
r
-path (from
x_{0}
to
x_{n}
)

if
d(x_{i-1},x_{i})\leq r
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
Two points
x,y\in X
are
r
-connected
if there is an
r
-path from
x
to
y
.
This defines an equivalence relation on
X
, whose equivalence classes are called
r
-components
.

Definition 12.2
.

Let
X,Y
be metric spaces,
f\colon X\to Y
, and
C\in(0,\infty)
.
We say that
f
is
C
-Lipschitz light
if

•

f
is
C
-Lipschitz, and

•

for every
E\subseteq Y
and
r\geq\operatorname{diam}E
, the
r
-components of
f^{-1}(E)

have diameter at most
Cr
.

We say that
f
is Lipschitz light if it is
C
-Lipschitz light for some
C\in(0,\infty)
.

Remark 12.3
.

12.2
differs from
[
CK13
, Definition 1.14]
,
but is equivalent for maps into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
; see
[
Dav21
, Section 1.3]
.

Remark 12.4
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be Lipschitz.
To see if
f
is Lipschitz light, it suffices to check if the second condition of
12.2

is satisfied for closed sets
E\subseteq Y

or, if
Y
is a normed space, for
E
closed and convex.
In particular, if
Y=\mathbb{R}
, it is enough to consider compact intervals.

Definition 12.5

(
[
CK13
, Definition 1.15]
)
.

The
Lipschitz dimension
of a metric space
X
is defined as

\dim_{L}X:=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\colon\text{there exists a Lipschitz light map }f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\}.

The following lemma will soon be useful.

Lemma 12.6
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
C_{1},\dots,C_{n}>0
there is a constant
C=C(C_{1},\dots,C_{n})>0
with the following property.
Let
X
be a metric space,
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\subseteq X
non-empty, and
r_{0}>0
.
Suppose that for
1\leq i\leq n
and
r\geq r_{0}
, every
r
-component of
A_{i}
has diameter at most
C_{i}r
.
Then, for
r\geq r_{0}
, every
r
-component of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
has diameter at most
Cr
.

Proof.

By induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it is enough to consider the case
n=2
.
Let
r\geq r_{0}
and
(x_{t})_{t=0}^{m}=(x(t))_{t=0}^{m}
be an
r
-path in
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
;
we need to show that
d(x_{0},x_{m})\leq C(C_{1},C_{2})r
.
We may suppose w.l.o.g.
x_{0}\in A_{1}
and
\{x_{t}\colon 0\leq t\leq m\}\cap A_{2}\neq\varnothing
.
Then, there is
k\in\mathbb{N}
and integers
0=t_{0}<\cdots<t_{k-1}<m=:t_{k}-1
such that, for
1\leq i\leq k
,

\{x_{t}\colon t_{i-1}\leq t<t_{i}\}\subseteq A_{1}
if
i
is odd and
\{x_{t}\colon t_{i-1}\leq t<t_{i}\}\subseteq A_{2}
otherwise.
That is,
t_{i}
is the time at which
(x_{t})_{t}
changes set.
In particular, for
1\leq i\leq k
,
(x_{t}\colon t_{i-1}\leq t<t_{i})
is an
r
-path fully contained in either
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
,
thus

(12.7)

d(x(t_{i-1}),x(t_{i}-1))\leq C_{0}r,

where
C_{0}:=\max(C_{1},C_{2})
.
Therefore, for
0\leq i<k/2-1
,

\displaystyle d(x(t_{2i}),x(t_{2i+2}))

\displaystyle\leq d(x(t_{2i}),x(t_{2i+1}-1))+d(x(t_{2i+1}-1),x(t_{2i+1}))+d(x(t_{2i+1}),x(t_{2i+2}-1))

\displaystyle\qquad+d(x(t_{2i+2}-1),x(t_{2i+2}))\leq 2(C_{0}+1)r;

that is,
(x(t_{2i})\colon 0\leq i<k/2-1)
is a
2(C_{0}+1)r
-path in
A_{1}
.
Hence, setting
T:=\max\{0\leq i\leq k-1\colon i\in 2\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
, we deduce

(12.8)

d(x(t_{0}),x(t_{T}))\leq 2C_{0}(C_{0}+1)r.

If
k
is odd, then
T=k-1
, and by
Eqs.

12.8
and
12.7
, we have

d(x_{0},x_{m})=d(x(t_{0}),x(t_{k}-1))\leq d(x(t_{0}),x(t_{k-1}))+d(x(t_{k-1}),x(t_{k}-1))\leq C_{0}(2C_{0}+3)r.

If
k
is even, then
T=k-2
, and we similarly obtain from
Eqs.

12.7
and
12.8

\displaystyle d(x_{0},x_{m})

\displaystyle\leq d(x(t_{0}),x(t_{k-2}))+d(x(t_{k-2}),x(t_{k-1}-1))+d(x(t_{k-1}-1),x(t_{k-1}))

\displaystyle\qquad+d(x(t_{k-1}),x(t_{k}-1))

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{0}(C_{0}+1)r+C_{0}r+r+C_{0}r=(2C_{0}^{2}+4C_{0}+1)r.

∎

Lemma 12.9
.

Let
(X,d_{\eta})
be a shortcut Laakso space.
For
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,y\in X
with
x(j)\neq y(j)
,
we have

\displaystyle d_{\eta}(x,y)

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{3}\min\big\{2d(h\{x,y\},W_{j}^{h}),2d(\{x,y\},\cup\mathcal{J}_{j})+\eta_{j}\delta_{j}\big\}.

Proof.

If
d_{\eta}(x,y)=d(x,y)
, by
6.4
we have

(12.10)

d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq 2d(h\{x,y\},W_{j}^{h}).

Suppose now
d_{\eta}(x,y)<d(x,y)
,
\epsilon>0
, and let
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
p_{-}\neq p_{+}\in S\in\mathcal{J}_{i}
be such that

(3+\epsilon)d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq d(x,p_{-})+\eta_{i}\delta_{i}+d(p_{+},y)
;
they exist by
3.11
and
6.25
.
If
i=j
, then

(12.11)

(3+\epsilon)d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq 2d(\{x,y\},\cup\mathcal{J}_{j})+\eta_{j}\delta_{j}.

If
i=j-1
, then
h(p_{\pm})\in\mathcal{J}_{i}^{h}\subseteq W_{j}^{h}
and so

(12.12)

(3+\epsilon)d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq 2d(h\{x,y\},W_{j}^{h}).

If
i\neq j-1,j
, then
p_{-}(j)=p_{+}(j)
and therefore either
x(j)\neq p_{\pm}(j)
or
y(j)\neq p_{\pm}(j)
.
Hence, by
6.4
, we have

(12.13)

(3+\epsilon)d_{\eta}(x,y)\geq\max\{d(x,p_{-}),d(p_{+},y)\}\geq 2d(h\{x,y\},W_{j}^{h})

Finally, combining
Eqs.

12.10
,
12.11
,
12.12
and
12.13
,
we have the thesis.
∎

Remark 12.14
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
.
Then, for
1\leq j\leq n
,
W_{j}^{h}\cap I=W_{j}^{h}\cap\partial I
,
and moreover
W_{n}^{h}\cap I\neq\varnothing
.

Lemma 12.15
.

Let
X
be a Laakso space.
For
n\geq 2
and
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
, it holds

\#\{l\in[n-1]\colon\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\cap I\neq\varnothing\}\leq 1.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}\subseteq[n-1]
be the set of the thesis,
suppose
\mathcal{L}\neq\varnothing
, and set
l:=\min\mathcal{L}
.
If
l=n-1
, then
\#\mathcal{L}=1
, so we may assume
n\geq 3
and
1\leq l\leq n-2
.
Since
\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\subseteq W_{l+1}^{h}
and
l+1<n
,
from
12.14
we deduce that
either
\mathcal{J}_{n-1}^{h}\cap\partial I=\varnothing
or
\mathcal{L}=\{l\}
.
Let
t\in I
be such that

\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\cap I=\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\cap\partial I=\{t\}
.
From the inclusion
\partial I\subseteq\{t-\tfrac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}},t,t+\tfrac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}}\}
,
it is then enough to show
t_{\pm}:=t\pm\tfrac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}}\notin\mathcal{J}_{n-1}^{h}
.
Write
t=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
with
t_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
0\leq t_{i}<N_{i}
,
and recall that

t\in\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}
is equivalent to
1\leq t_{l}<N_{l}-1
,
t_{l+1}=N_{l+1}/2
, and
t_{i}=0
for
l+2\leq i\leq n
.
Define
t_{\pm,i}
in the analogous way.
Then
t_{n}=0
implies
t_{+,n}=1<N_{n}/2
, proving
t_{+}\notin\mathcal{J}_{n-1}^{h}
.
For
t_{-}
, we have

\displaystyle t_{-}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}+\frac{N_{l+1}/2-1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{l+1}}+\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{l+1}}-\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}+\frac{N_{l+1}/2-1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{l+1}}+\sum_{i=l+2}^{n}\frac{N_{i}-1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}

and so
t_{-,n}=N_{n}-1>N_{n}/2
, proving
t_{-}\notin\mathcal{J}_{n-1}^{h}
.
∎

Lemma 12.16
.

Let
X
be a Laakso space.
Let
n\geq 2
,
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
l\in[n-1]
and suppose
\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\cap I\neq\varnothing
.
Then
W_{l}^{h}\cap I=\varnothing
.

Proof.

From
12.14
we see that,
if
n\geq 3
and
l+1<n
, then
\varnothing\neq\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\cap I\subseteq W_{l+1}^{h}\cap\partial I

and so
\partial I\setminus\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\subseteq W_{n}^{h}
.
Suppose
n\geq 2
,
l=n-1
, and let
t\in I
be such that
\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\cap\partial I=\{t\}
.
Arguing as in
Lemma

12.15
, it is not
difficult to see that
t\pm\tfrac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}}\notin W_{n-1}^{h}
.
Since
\partial I\subseteq\{t-\tfrac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}},t,t+\tfrac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}}\}
,
this implies
\varnothing=W_{n-1}^{h}\cap\partial I=W_{n-1}^{h}\cap I
and concludes the proof.
∎

Notation 12.17
.

Let
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
be a Laakso space and
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\geq 2
, and
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
set

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{W}(I)

\displaystyle:=\{l\in[n-1]\colon W_{l}^{h}\cap I\neq\varnothing\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\{l\in[n-1]\colon\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\cap I\neq\varnothing\text{ and }\eta_{l}\delta_{l}\leq|I|\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(I,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{L}_{W}(I)\cup\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta),

and recall that
\mathcal{L}_{W}(I),\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)
are disjoint and contain at most one element each;
see
Lemmas

12.15
and
12.16
.
For
a\in[M]^{n-1}
,
let
A(I,\eta;a)
denote the set of all
b\in[M]^{n-1}
for which, setting

\Delta:=\{1\leq j\leq n-1\colon a(j)\neq b(j)\},

we have
\Delta\subseteq\mathcal{L}(I,\eta)
and,
if
\Delta\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)\neq\varnothing
, then

1=a(j)(=b(j))

for
\min(\Delta\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta))<j\leq n-1
with
j\notin\mathcal{L}_{W}(I)
.
The set
A(I,\eta;a)
is well-defined even if
\mathcal{L}(I,\eta)=\varnothing
,
and it always contains
a
.
Moreover,
\{(a,b)\in[M]^{n-1}\times[M]^{n-1}\colon a\in A(I,\eta;b)\}

is an equivalence relation on
[M]^{n-1}
,
therefore

\mathcal{A}_{\eta}(I):=\{A(I,\eta;a)\colon a\in[M]^{n-1}\}

is a partition of
[M]^{n-1}
.
For
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}(I)
, we define

E_{A}:=\bigcup_{a\in A}Q_{I,a}.

Lemma 12.18
.

Let
(X,d_{\eta})
be a shortcut Laakso space.
For
n\geq 2
,
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
, and
A_{1}\neq A_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}(I)
,
we have

d_{\eta}(E_{A_{1}},E_{A_{2}})\geq\tfrac{1}{3}|I|.

Proof.

Let
a_{i}\in A_{i}
, and define
\Delta
as in
12.17
, but with
a_{1},a_{2}
in place of
a,b
.
Since
A_{1}\neq A_{2}
, we have
\Delta\neq\varnothing
and
a_{1}\notin A(I,\eta;a_{2})
.
Let
x_{i}\in Q_{I,a_{i}}
,
1\leq i\leq 2
, and observe that by definition of
Q_{I,a_{i}}
,
we have
x_{i}(j)=a_{i}(j)
for
1\leq j\leq n-1
.
Suppose first
\Delta\nsubseteq\mathcal{L}(I,\eta)
, so that there is
1\leq j\leq n-1
,
j\notin\mathcal{L}(I,\eta)
, such that
x_{1}(j)\neq x_{2}(j)
.
Since
j\notin\mathcal{L}(I,\eta)
,
W_{j}^{h}\cap I=\varnothing
and either
\eta_{j}\delta_{j}>|I|
or
\mathcal{J}_{j}^{h}\cap I=\varnothing
.
Then

\displaystyle d(h\{x,y\},W_{j}^{h})

\displaystyle\geq d(I,W_{\leq n}^{h}\setminus I)\geq|I|,

\displaystyle 2d(\{x,y\},\cup\mathcal{J}_{j})+\eta_{j}\delta_{j}

\displaystyle\geq 2d(I,\mathcal{J}_{j}^{h})+\eta_{j}\delta_{j}\geq|I|,

and, by
Lemma

12.9
, we have

(12.19)

d_{\eta}(x_{1},x_{2})\geq\tfrac{1}{3}|I|.

Suppose now
\Delta\subseteq\mathcal{L}(I,\eta)
,
i.e.
x_{1}(j)=x_{2}(j)
for
1\leq j\leq n-1
with
j\notin\mathcal{L}(I,\eta)
.
Then
\Delta\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)\neq\varnothing
,

\Delta\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)=\{l\}
for some
l\in[n-1]
(by
Lemma

12.15
),
and there is
l<j\leq n-1
with
j\notin\mathcal{L}_{W}(I)

and
x_{1}(j)=x_{2}(j)\neq 1
.
Since
l\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)
, by
Lemma

12.16
we have
W_{l}^{h}\cap I=\varnothing
and therefore
d(W_{l}^{h},I)\geq|I|
.
Since
l\in\Delta
and
l\leq n-1
, we have
x_{1}(l)=a_{1}(l)\neq a_{2}(l)=x_{2}(l)
and
Lemma

12.9
gives

\displaystyle d_{\eta}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{3}\min\{2d(h\{x_{1},x_{2}\},W_{l}^{h}),2d(\{x_{1},x_{2}\},\cup\mathcal{J}_{l})+\eta_{l}\delta_{l}\}

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{2}{3}\min\{|I|,d(\{x_{1},x_{2}\},\cup\mathcal{J}_{l})\}

Let
x\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
and
y\in\cup\mathcal{J}_{l}
.
The inequality
l<j
implies
x(j)\neq 1=y(j)
, and
6.4
gives

d(x,y)\geq d(h(x),W_{j}^{h})\geq|I|,

because
I\cap W_{j}^{h}=\varnothing
.
Hence

(12.20)

d_{\eta}(x_{1},x_{2})\geq\tfrac{2}{3}|I|.

Since
a_{i}\in A_{i}
and
x_{i}\in Q_{I,a_{i}}
were arbitrary,
Eq.

12.19
and
Eq.

12.20
conclude the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.21
.

Let
(X,d_{\eta})
be a shortcut Laakso space.
For
n\geq 2
,
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
, and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}(I)
, we have

\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}(E_{A})\leq 5|I|.

Proof.

Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
and
x_{i}\in Q_{I,a_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq 2
and define
\Delta
as in
12.17
, with
a_{1},a_{2}
in place of
a,b
.
Recall that
W_{l}^{h}\cap I\neq\varnothing
for
l\geq n
.
Thus, if
\Delta\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)=\varnothing
, then
\Delta\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{W}(I)
and so
I
contains all wormhole heights necessary to travel from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
,
which, by
6.4
, gives

d_{\eta}(x_{1},x_{2})\leq d(x_{1},x_{2})\leq 2|I|.

Suppose now
\Delta\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)\neq\varnothing
; in particular
a_{1}\neq a_{2}
.
By
Lemma

12.15
,
there is
1\leq l\leq n-1
such that
\Delta\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)=\{l\}
.
Let
t\in\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\cap I
and let
p_{1},p_{2}\in X
be given by
h(p_{1})=h(p_{2})=t
,

p_{i}(j)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}x_{i}(j),&1\leq j\leq l\text{ and }j\notin\mathcal{L}_{W}(I)\\
1,&l+2\leq j<\infty\text{ or }j\in\mathcal{L}_{W}(I)\end{array}\right.,

where we have not prescribed the
(l+1)
-th digit because
h(p_{i})\in\mathcal{J}_{l}^{h}\subseteq W_{l+1}^{h}
.
Since
\Delta\subseteq\mathcal{L}(I,\eta)
by assumption, it follows that
p_{1},p_{2}
agree at all digits except
possibly the
l
-th one.
Hence, there is
S\in\mathcal{J}_{l}
for which
p_{1},p_{2}\in S
, and so

d_{\eta}(p_{1},p_{2})\leq\eta_{l}\delta_{l}\leq|I|
by definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{J}}(I,\eta)
.
By definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\eta}(I)
, it follows that
x_{i},p_{i}
may differ only
at digits
j\geq n
or
j\in\mathcal{L}_{W}(I)
.
Thus, all wormhole heights necessary to travel from
x_{i}
to
p_{i}
are contained in
I
,
and
6.4
gives

d_{\eta}(x_{i},p_{i})\leq d(x_{i},p_{i})\leq 2|I|.

The thesis then follows by triangle inequality.
∎

Lemma 12.22
.

Let
(X,d_{\eta})
be a shortcut Laakso space.
Then there is
C\geq 1

such that for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
, and
r\geq|I|
,
the
r
-components of
h^{-1}(I)
in
(X,d_{\eta})
have diameter at most
Cr
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
.

The constant
C
depends only on
\max_{n}N_{n}
, where
(N_{n})
is such that
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
for some
M
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

12.18
and
Lemma

12.21
, there is a universal constant
C_{1}\geq 1
such that
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\geq 2
, and
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
, the sets
\{E_{A}\colon A\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}(I)\}
are
C_{1}|I|
-bounded and strictly
C_{1}^{-1}|I|
-separated in
(X,d_{\eta})
.
Since

h^{-1}(I)=\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}(I)}E_{A},

the strict separation shows that any
C_{1}^{-1}|I|
-path must be contained in a single set
E_{A}
, for some
A\in\mathcal{A}_{\eta}(I)
.
Hence, the
C_{1}^{-1}|I|
-components of
h^{-1}(I)
in
(X,d_{\eta})
have diameter at most
C_{1}|I|
w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
.

Let
0<r\leq C_{1}^{-1}\delta_{2}
, where
\delta_{i}=\frac{1}{N_{1}\cdots N_{i}}
is as in
6.17
, and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be the greatest integer
for which
r\leq C_{1}^{-1}\delta_{k}
.
By maximality, we have

(12.23)

C_{1}^{-1}\delta_{k+1}<r\leq C_{1}^{-1}\delta_{k}

and
k\geq 2
.
Let
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
, with
r\geq|I|
.
Then
n\geq 2
and there is
J\in\mathcal{I}_{k}
with
I\subseteq J
.
Since
h^{-1}(I)\subseteq h^{-1}(J)
, each
r
-component of
h^{-1}(I)
is contained in an
r
-component of
h^{-1}(J)
,
which in turn is contained in a
C_{1}^{-1}|J|
-component of
h^{-1}(J)
, because
r\leq C_{1}^{-1}|J|
.
This shows that every
r
-component of
h^{-1}(I)
has diameter w.r.t.
d_{\eta}
at most

C_{1}|J|\leq C_{1}^{2}N_{k+1}r\leq C_{1}^{2}C_{2}r,

where we have used
Eq.

12.23
and
set

C_{2}:=\max_{n}N_{n}
, which (exists and) is finite by
Eq.

6.1
.
If
r>C_{1}^{-1}\delta_{2}
and
I\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
,
the
r
-components of
h^{-1}(I)
have diameter at most

\operatorname{diam}_{\eta}X\leq 2\leq 2C_{1}\delta_{2}^{-1}r\leq 2C_{1}C_{2}^{2}r
.
∎

Lemma 12.24
.

Let
(X,d_{\eta})
be a shortcut Laakso space.
Then there is
C\geq 1
such that the map
h\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to\mathbb{R}
is
C
-Lipschitz light.

The constant
C
depends only on
\max_{n}N_{n}
, where
(N_{n})
is such that
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
for some
M
.

Proof.

We already know that
\operatorname{LIP}_{\eta}(h)=\operatorname{LIP}(h)=1
.
Let
C_{1}\geq 1
be the constant of
Lemma

12.22
.
It is not difficult to see that there is an integer
N\geq 2
, depending only on
\max_{n}N_{n}
,
with the following property.
For every interval
J\subseteq[0,1]

there are
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
J_{1},\dots,J_{N}\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
satisfying

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}J_{i},

\displaystyle|J_{i}|\leq|J|

for
1\leq i\leq N
.
(We can take
N=\max_{n}N_{n}+2
.)
It is now easy to conclude.
Let
J\subseteq[0,1]
be an interval,
r\geq|J|
, and
J_{1},\dots,J_{N}
as above.
Since
J_{i}\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
and
r\geq|J|\geq|J_{i}|
,
Lemma

12.22
shows
that the
r
-components of
h^{-1}(J_{i})
have diameter at most
C_{1}r
.
By
Lemma

12.6
, there is a constant
C\geq 1
, depending only on
C_{1}
and
N
,
such that the
r
-components of
h^{-1}(J)
have diameter at most
Cr
.
Hence, from
12.4
,
h
is
C
-Lipschitz light.
Since
C_{1}
and
N
depend only on
\max_{n}N_{n}
, so does
C
.
∎

The notion of Nagata dimension was introduced in
[
Ass82
]
, see also
[
LS05
]
.
For our purposes, it is enough to know that

(12.25)

\dim_{T}Z\leq\dim_{N}Z\leq\dim_{L}Z,

where
Z
is a compact metric space and

\dim_{T}Z
,
\dim_{N}Z
denote respectively its topological and Nagata dimension.
See
[
LS05
, Theorem 2.2]
for the first inequality and
[
Dav21
, Corollary 3.5]
for the second.

Proposition 12.26
.

Let
(X,d_{\eta})
be a shortcut Laakso space.
Then

\dim_{T}(X,d_{\eta})=\dim_{N}(X,d_{\eta})=\dim_{L}(X,d_{\eta})=1.

Proof.

By
Lemma

12.24
, we have
\dim_{L}(X,d_{\eta})\leq 1
.
Since
(X,d_{\eta})
contains a homeomorphic copy of
[0,1]
, we also have
\dim_{T}(X,d_{\eta})\geq 1
.
Finally,
Eq.

12.25
concludes the proof.
∎

As a corollary, we have the following.

Proposition 12.27
.

Let
(X,d_{\eta})
be a shortcut Laakso space. There are
C\geq 1

and a map
f\colon X\to L^{1}([0,1])
satisfying

d_{\eta}(x,y)\leq\|f(x)-f(y)\|_{L^{1}([0,1])}\leq Cd_{\eta}(x,y),

for
x,y\in X
.

The constant
C
depends only on
\max_{n}N_{n}
, where
(N_{n})
is such that
X=X(M;(N_{n}))
for some
M
.

Proof.

Let
C_{1}\geq 1
be the constant of
Lemma

12.24
.
By
[
CK13
, Theorem 1.11]
(with e.g. constant
m=2
) and
Lemma

12.24
,
(X,d_{\eta})
admits a biLipschitz
embedding into an admissible inverse system
[
CK13
, Definition 1.8]
,
with biLipschitz constant depending only on
C_{1}
.
But then, by
[
CK13
, Theorem 1.16]
, there are a measure space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\nu)

and a biLipschitz embedding
\tilde{f}\colon(X,d_{\eta})\to L^{1}(\nu)
,
with biLipschitz constant depending only on
C_{1}
.
Since the closed span
Y
of
\tilde{f}(X)
is a separable subspace of
L^{1}(\nu)
,
by
[
Ost13
, Fact 1.20]
,
there is a linear isometry
\iota\colon Y\to L^{1}([0,1])
.
Finally, setting
f:=\iota\circ\tilde{f}
concludes the proof.
∎

We now turn to non-embeddability in Banach spaces with RNP.
Note that, if
\eta
is chosen such that
(X,d_{\eta},\mathcal{H}^{s})
is purely PI unrectifiable, we cannot appeal to the differentiability theory
of Cheeger and Kleiner
[
CK09
]
, at least not directly.
Nonetheless, the controlled behaviour of the contraction
\eta
(
Lemma

3.18
) paired with
a weak Sard theorem for Lipschitz functions on LDS (
[
Dav15
, Theorem 1.4]
)
is sufficient to rule out DS-regular embeddability.
Indeed, this is implied by the following more general result.

Proposition 12.28
.

Let
Y
be a Banach space,

(X,d,\mu)
an
s
-ADR
Y
-LDS with shortcuts, and
\eta=(\eta_{i})_{i}\subseteq(0,1]
.
Then

\mathcal{H}^{s}_{Y}(f(E))=0

for every
\mu
-measurable set
E\subseteq X
and Lipschitz
f\colon(E,d_{\eta})\to Y
.

In particular,
no positive-measure
\mu
-measurable subset of
(X,d_{\eta})
admits a David-Semmes regular embedding in
Y
.

Proof.

First, let us verify that if
(U,\varphi\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a chart in
(X,d,\mu)
with
\mu(U)>0
,
then
n<s
.
To see this, suppose not.
Then
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n=s
(by
[
Sch16a
, Theorem 5.99]
or
[
BKO23
, Theorem 5.3]
)
and
(U,d)
is
s
-rectifiable by
[
BL17
]
, but by
3.32

it has a Lipschitz image with positive measure and no biLipschitz piece,
which is not possible by Kirchheim’s differentiation theorem
[
Kir94
]
and
\mu(U)>0
.

We now turn to the proof of the statement.
It is enough to consider the case where
\mu(E)>0
and there is
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\varphi\colon(X,d)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

so that
(E,\varphi)
is a chart of
(X,d,\mu)
.
If
f\colon(E,d_{\eta})\to Y
is Lipschitz, then so is
f\colon(E,d)\to Y
,
and
[
Dav15
, Theorem 1.4]
gives
\mathcal{H}^{s}(f(E))=0
,
as claimed.
Note that the proof of
[
Dav15
, Theorem 1.4]

generalises verbatim to our setting.

Finally, suppose
E\subseteq X
is
\mu
-measurable and
f\colon(E,d_{\eta})\to Y
is David-Semmes regular.
Then, by
Lemma

3.18
,
f\colon(E,d)\to Y
is also DS-regular
and so (by
Eq.

3.16
) we have

\mu(E)\sim\mathcal{H}^{s}_{Y}(f(E))=0,

concluding the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Metric spaces with shortcuts uniformly and Carnot groups

A.1.
Comparison with
[
LDLR17
]

Our definition of metric spaces with shortcuts (
3.1
)
is inspired by
[
LDLR17
]
,
where the following condition is introduced (without giving it a name).

Definition A.1
.

We say that a metric space
X
has
shortcuts
\delta
-uniformly
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, if
for
x_{0}\in X
and
0<r<\operatorname{diam}X
there is a
\delta r
-separated set
S\subseteq U(x_{0},r)
with
\#S\geq 2

satisfying

d(x,y)\leq d(x,z)+d(w,y)

for
x,y\notin U(x_{0},r)
and
z,w\in S
.

A.1
differs slightly from

[
LDLR17
, Equation (1.1)]

in that we allow for sets
S
with
\#S>2
.
As pointed out in
3.3
, this is only a minor change.
Metric spaces as in
A.1

appeared also in

[
JR17
]
,
where they are called spaces with
\delta
-invisible pieces.

Under mild assumptions,
A.1
implies the existence of shortcuts as in
3.1

(see
Lemma

A.2
), but

3.1
allows for more flexibility,
since the scales
(\delta_{i})
are allowed to decay arbitrarily quickly.

Recall that a metric space
X
is
C
-uniformly perfect
,
C>1
,
if
U(x,r)\setminus U(x,r/C)\neq\varnothing
for
x\in X
and
0<r<\operatorname{diam}X
,
and uniformly perfect if it there is
C>1
as above.
Examples of uniformly perfect metric spaces include connected and ADR metric spaces.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
X
be a
C
-uniformly perfect metric space with shortcuts
\delta_{0}
-uniformly.
Then
X
has shortcuts (
3.1
).

More precisely, for

0<\delta\leq\delta_{0}/16C
,
0<A<\operatorname{diam}X/4\delta
,
and
M\geq 4C+5
,

X
has shortcuts with
parameters
\delta_{i}=A\delta^{i}
,

a=b=2
,
a_{0}=\delta_{0}
, and
M
as above.

Lemma

A.2
follows arguing as in
[
LDLR17
, Section 3.1]
.
We provide a complete proof since our setting differs slightly and we need to keep track of the several constants (
a_{0},a,b
, and
M
).

Lemma A.3
.

Let
X
be a
C
-uniformly perfect metric space,
0<R<\operatorname{diam}X
, and
\mathcal{N}_{0}\subseteq X
an
R
-separated set.
Then, for
0<r\leq R/4C
,
any
r
-net
\mathcal{N}
of
X\setminus U(\mathcal{N}_{0},r)

is a
(C+1)r
-net of
X
, i.e.
B(\mathcal{N},(C+1)r)=X
.

Proof.

Since
X
is
C
-uniformly perfect, we have

U(x,\rho)\setminus U(y,t)\neq\varnothing
for
x,y\in X
,
0<\rho<\operatorname{diam}X
, and
0<t<\rho/C
.
Let
Cr<\rho\leq R/2-r
and pick
x\in X
.
Since
\mathcal{N}_{0}
is
R
-separated, the choice of
\rho
implies that there is at most one
y\in\mathcal{N}_{0}

such that
U(x,\rho)\cap U(y,r)\neq\varnothing
.
Hence, there is
y_{0}\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
such that
U(x,\rho)\setminus U(\mathcal{N}_{0},r)=U(x,\rho)\setminus U(y_{0},r)
,
which is then non-empty because
r<\rho/C
.
Since
X\setminus U(\mathcal{N}_{0},r)\subseteq B(\mathcal{N},r)
, we have

d(x,\mathcal{N})<\rho+r
for every
x\in X
and
\rho
as above,
proving
B(\mathcal{N},(C+1)r)=X
.
∎

Remark A.4
.

The conclusion of
Lemma

A.3
characterises uniformly perfect metric spaces.
Indeed, taking
x\in X
,
\mathcal{N}_{0}=\{x\}
,
0<R<\operatorname{diam}X
, and
r=R/4C
,
we find
y\in X
with
r\leq d(x,y)<4Cr
, i.e.
y\in U(x,R)\setminus U(x,R/4C)
.

Proof of
Lemma

A.2
.

We first construct
\mathcal{J}_{1}
.
Let
\mathcal{N}_{1}\subseteq X
be a maximal
4A\delta
-separated set
and,
for
x\in\mathcal{N}_{1}
, let
S_{x}\subseteq U(x,A\delta)
be as in
A.1

with
2\leq\#S_{x}\leq M
.
Set
\mathcal{J}_{1}:=\{S_{x}\colon x\in\mathcal{N}_{1}\}
.
It is then not difficult to see that
\mathcal{J}_{1}
satisfies the conditions of
3.1

with the current values of
a_{0},a,b,M
, and
\delta_{1}=A\delta
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\geq 2
, and suppose we have found
\mathcal{J}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{J}_{n-1}
satisfying
the conditions of
3.1
with
a_{0},a,b,M
as above and
\delta_{i}=A\delta^{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
.
Set
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n-1}\bigcup\mathcal{J}_{i}
,
let
\mathcal{N}_{n}\subseteq X\setminus U(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}},4A\delta^{n})
be a maximal
4A\delta^{n}
-separated set,
and, for
x\in\mathcal{N}_{n}
, let
S_{x}\subseteq U(x,A\delta^{n})
be as in
A.1
with
2\leq\#S_{x}\leq M
.
Set
\mathcal{J}_{n}:=\{S_{x}\colon x\in\mathcal{N}_{n}\}
.
Observe that
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
is
A\delta_{0}\delta^{n-1}
-separated,
A\delta_{0}\delta^{n-1}<\operatorname{diam}X
,

4A\delta^{n}\leq(A\delta_{0}\delta^{n-1})/4C
,
and hence, by
Lemma

A.3
,

\mathcal{N}_{n}\neq\varnothing
and

B(\mathcal{N}_{n},(C+1)4A\delta^{n})=X
.
Since
\mathcal{N}_{n}\subseteq U(\cup\mathcal{J}_{n},A\delta^{n})
,

\mathcal{J}_{n}
satisfies also the last condition of

3.1
(with
M
as above).
Lastly, it is not difficult to verify that
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},A\delta^{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
satisfies the first four conditions
of
3.1
with
\delta_{n}=A\delta^{n}
.
∎

Remark A.5
.

Let
X
be a metric space as
Lemma

A.2
and suppose there is
N\geq 2

such that the sets
S
as in
A.1
may be taken with
\#S=N
.
It follows from the proof of
Lemma

A.2
that we can then construct
\{(\mathcal{J}_{i},A\delta^{i})\}_{i\geq 1}

as in the thesis (with
M\geq\max(N,4C+5)
), which additionally satisfies
\#S=N
for
S\in\mathcal{J}=\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{J}_{i}
.

A.2.
Carnot groups have shortcuts

Carnot groups are a distinguished class of Lie groups, whose underlying smooth manifold may be identified with a finite dimensional Euclidean space.
We refer the reader to
[
LD17
,
SC16
,
BLU07
,
Don24
]

for more information on the topic.
From our point of view, Carnot groups are a class of ADR
1
-PI spaces which can be equipped
with a distance with shortcuts compatible with
the Cheeger differentiable structure.
We will recall the few basic facts we need.

We identify Carnot groups with their Lie algebra via exponential coordinates (see
[
BLU07
, Proposition 2.2.22]
)
and write
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{k}}
,
where
\mathbb{R}^{m_{i}}
corresponds to the
i
-th layer of the stratification.
We denote points of
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{k}}
as
x=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(k)})

and define
\Delta_{\lambda}\colon\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G}
,

\Delta_{\lambda}x:=(\lambda x^{(1)},\dots,\lambda^{k}x^{(k)})
,
for
\lambda>0
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
.
The dilations
\Delta_{\lambda}
are group automorphisms; in particular,

\Delta_{\lambda}(x\cdot y)=\Delta_{\lambda}x\cdot\Delta_{\lambda}y

and
\Delta_{\lambda}(x^{-1})=(\Delta_{\lambda}x)^{-1}
,
where
\cdot
denotes the group law.
Also, the identity element
e_{\mathbb{G}}
of
\mathbb{G}
satisfies
\Delta_{\lambda}e_{\mathbb{G}}=e_{\mathbb{G}}
for
\lambda>0
,
proving
e_{\mathbb{G}}=0=(0,\dots,0)
.
A distance
d\colon\mathbb{G}\times\mathbb{G}\to[0,\infty)
is
invariant
if it satisfies

d(z\cdot x,z\cdot y)=d(x,y),\qquad d(\Delta_{\lambda}x,\Delta_{\lambda}y)=\lambda d(x,y),

for
x,y,z\in\mathbb{G}
and
\lambda>0
.
Note that we do not require
d
to induce the (Euclidean) topology of
\mathbb{G}
,
since this follows from the definition, see
[
LDG21
, Theorem 1.1]
.
Invariant distances are closely related to

homogeneous norms
, namely functions
N\colon\mathbb{G}\to[0,\infty)
such that

(A.6)

\displaystyle N(x)

\displaystyle=0\text{ implies }x=0,

\displaystyle N(x^{-1})

\displaystyle=N(x),

\displaystyle N(\Delta_{\lambda}x)

\displaystyle=\lambda N(x),

\displaystyle N(x\cdot y)

\displaystyle\leq N(x)+N(y),

for
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
and
\lambda>0
.
Indeed, if
d
is an invariant distance, then
d(\cdot,0)
is a homogeneous norm
and, conversely, if
N
is a homogeneous norm, then
d(x,y):=N(y^{-1}\cdot x)
defines an invariant distance.
In particular, homogeneous norms are continuous and one may verify that they are all equivalent, i.e. for any two homogeneous norms
N_{1},N_{2}

there is a constant
C\geq 1
such that
C^{-1}N_{1}\leq N_{2}\leq CN_{1}
;
see
[
BLU07
, Proposition 5.1.4]
7
7

7

Note that,
following
[
SC16
, Definition 2.6]
,
our definition of homogeneous norm is more restrictive than
[
BLU07
, Definition 5.1.1]
.
Also, we do not explicitly require continuity, since
it follows from the other conditions; see
[
LDG21
, Theorem 1.1]
.
.
It follows that all invariant distances are biLipschitz equivalent.

Proposition A.7
.

Let
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{k}}
be a Carnot group of step
k\geq 2
and
\delta\in(0,1/2)
.
Then there is an invariant distance
d
on
\mathbb{G}
such that

(A.8)

d(x,y)\leq d(x,z)+d(w,y),

for
x,y\notin U_{d}(0,1)
and
z,w\in B_{d}(0,\delta)
with
z^{(1)}=w^{(1)}
.

By left-translation and dilation,
Eq.

A.8
implies

d(x,y)\leq d(x,z)+d(w,y),

whenever there are
x_{0}\in\mathbb{G}
,
r>0
such that

x,y\notin U_{d}(x_{0},r)
and
z,w\in B_{d}(x_{0},\delta r)
with
z^{(1)}=w^{(1)}
.

The proof of
A.7
relies on elementary estimates
which already appeared in
[
FSSC03
, Theorem 5.1]
,
which we follow closely.
They are based on a few properties of the group laws of Carnot groups, which we now recall; see
[
BLU07
, Remark 1.4.4]
.
For any Carnot group
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{k}}
,
there are polynomial functions
\mathcal{Q}^{(i)}\colon\mathbb{G}\times\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}^{m_{i}}

such that the group law may be written as

(A.9)

(x\cdot y)^{(i)}=x^{(i)}+y^{(i)}+\mathcal{Q}^{(i)}(x,y)

and, moreover,
\mathcal{Q}^{(i)}(x,y)
depends only on
x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(i-1)},y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(i-1)}
; in particular,
\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}=0
.
From this, one may verify by induction that
(x^{-1})^{(i)}=-x^{(i)}
for
1\leq i\leq k
,
i.e.
x^{-1}=-x=(-x^{(1)},\dots,-x^{(k)})
.

Lemma A.10
.

Let
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m_{k}}
be a Carnot group of step
k\geq 2
and, for
1\leq i\leq k
,
let
\|\cdot\|_{i}
be a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{m_{i}}
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)},\dots,\mathcal{Q}^{(k)}
be as in
Eq.

A.9
and
set
\|x\|_{\mathbb{G},i}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq i}\|x^{(j)}\|_{j}^{\frac{1}{j}}
for
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
1\leq i\leq k
.
Then, there is a constant
C>0
, such that for
2\leq i\leq k
and
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
, we have

\|\mathcal{Q}^{(i)}(x,y)\|_{i}\leq C\max_{1\leq j\leq i-1}\|x\|_{\mathbb{G},i-1}^{j}\|y\|_{\mathbb{G},i-1}^{i-j}.

Proof.

This lemma is essentially extracted from the proof of
[
FSSC03
, Theorem 5.1]
.
Set
h_{0}:=0
,
h_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{i}m_{j}
for
1\leq i\leq k
, and define

|\sigma|_{\mathbb{G}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{l=h_{i-1}+1}^{h_{i}}i\,\sigma_{l}
,
for
